A.21. Executive summary (max 50 words)

1. Vanuatu leads the world disaster risk index as the country with the highest disaster score of 49.74, indicating it is the most highly exposed to natural hazards.* A global analysis of countries affected by the impacts of climate change shows that Vanuatu has experienced the largest increase in disaster losses globally, based on losses per GDP percentage between 1990 and 2018*. These risks are evident in the recent events such as TC Harold (2015), TC Pam (2015), TC Winston (2016), TC Harold (2016), marine heatwave (2016), TC Hola (2018), TC Harold (2020) and regular flooding during heavy rainfall events. These climate change impacts have had a significant impact on the livelihoods of Vanuatu’s people, with loss of income and food security being major concerns.


---
**Page 7**
---

The current available evidence indicates that the most significant climate change challenges facing communities in Vanuatu are due to increasing frequency and/or intensity of extreme events:

1. Temperature: air and sea (minimum, mean and maximum daily temperatures) and events such as heatwaves.
2. Rainfall patterns (increased duration of dry periods, changing frequency and intensity of extreme rainfall events).
3. Tropical cyclones (increased intensity including severe wind and waves, and intense rainfall and flooding).

The vulnerability of communities in Vanuatu to these climate hazards is driven by the country's geography and heavy dependence on natural resources for subsistence and income. Other factors that contribute include: a high proportion of the population living below the poverty line; limited access to health services (recently graduated from LDC status, but likely to be pushed back to it by the economic impacts of COVID-19); and a young and growing population with a high dependency ratio. Small-scale and dispersed population often isolated during disaster events (due to the country's vast ocean territory, with widely scattered islands).

The Vanuatu Community-based Climate Resilience Project (VCCRP) will support highly vulnerable rural communities across 29 Area Councils in Vanuatu to increase their resilience to climate variability and change by:

1. Improving access to information on climate variability and change.
2. Building community capacity for adaptation planning, implementation and monitoring.
3. Strengthening local governance structures that ensure rural populations are able to adapt to changing climate trends and reduce disaster risk.

The project will achieve this goal via activities in three interlinked components:

1. Given the limited resources available and strong emphasis on building local resilience to climate change impacts, including by providing access to climate information and early warning.
2. Scalable, locally appropriate actions are implemented to meet community adaptation needs to create a more resilient future for all Vanuatu communities (adaptation package).
3. Institutional adaptive capacity is enhanced by building national-level systems (adaptation package) at the local level and enhancing local-provincial-national linkages.

The project will directly reach 97,157 people in 19,256 households across 282 communities across all six provinces (30% of the total population of Vanuatu) and further 110,000 people indirectly (a further 40% of the total population or 52% of the rural population). By working directly with community members, the project will ensure that adaptation actions are tailored to meet local needs and priorities in communities. Further, the adaptation package approach gives communities opportunities to select the most appropriate adaptation measures for their specific situation, which is a key part of the solution that minimizes vulnerability and build on local knowledge, skills and innovation.

# B.1. Climate context (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

## Background

### Vulnerability and Adaptation

6\. The Vanuatu Climate Change and Disaster Risk Reduction Policy (2016-2030) states that the country is "one of the most vulnerable countries in the world to climate change and disaster risks", while the 2009 World Bank report on climate change vulnerability ranked Vanuatu as one of the top five most vulnerable.

7\. Vanuatu is characterized by mostly mountainous volcanic islands with steep catchments leading to numerous rivers, streams, and waterfalls. The country experiences a tropical maritime climate where the climate can vary considerably each year due to the El Niño Southern Oscillation (ENSO) cycle and other drivers. Vanuatu has experienced at least one cyclone every year since 1970.

8\. Vanuatu has experienced at least one cyclone every year and substantial impacts, with two category five intensity cyclones in recent years. The country is also vulnerable to droughts, floods, landslides, and tsunamis. The most significant economic sectors are agriculture, forestry, fishing, and tourism. The agricultural sector accounts for 20% of the GDP (approximately USD $150 million). Vanuatu has also exposed to geological hazards—earthquakes, tsunamis, and volcanic eruptions.

9\. The country's population of approximately 272,000 (2016 mini census) is spread over 88 islands and atolls. Approximately 54% live on the main island of Efate, while 34% reside in the outer islands within 13 km of the coast and 60% living within 1 km of the coast. Coastal environments and natural resources play a vital role in the livelihoods of coastal communities.

10\. The majority of Vanuatu's population (approximately 85%) lives on small islands, with a high dependence on natural resources stems from the fact that 65% of the 83 islands that make up the Vanuatu archipelago are less than two square kilometers in size. This makes it difficult for people to acquire a profound understanding of their land, sea and climate. Generational knowledge combined with the expertise that results from being primary managers and beneficiaries of natural resources is a key element in building resilience against climate change impacts and disasters.

11\. However, an increasingly unpredictable climate is challenging the limits of local and traditional knowledge and practices, making it difficult to predict future conditions for the future. This is further compounded by a likely future in which extremes (like tropical cyclones) are more intense, frequent, and longer lasting. The development of new tools such as early warning systems, community and livelihoods strategies will help offset the impacts of these extremes.

12\. National climate change policy and strategy are developed under the oversight of all climate matters through the Ministry of Climate Change. Sub-national governance in Vanuatu consists of six Provincial Government Councils which is sub-divided into Area Councils. As of 2021, there are 71 Area Councils across the country. The Area Councils are further divided into Local Councils and Village Councils. Area Councils have a varying number of communities (averaging around 9), and each community has its own village council. The village councils are responsible for implementing both adaptation and disaster preparedness and response. In practice, many communities do not have a dedicated climate change focal person or committee. However, the majority of communities have responded positively to climate change challenges from a governance perspective and has established the National Advisory Committee on Climate Change (NACC) as an umbrella body that provides guidance and direction for responsibility for coordinating and mainstreaming climate change policies, programs and projects and living with climate change.

13\. While CDDCCGs are the primary mechanism for adaptation planning at the local level, they have no independent legal status. They are established by mandate from the MoCC's National Disaster Management Authority (NDMA) under the Climate Change and Disaster Risk Reduction Policy 2016-2030 as outlined in the MiCC publication CDBRR Handbook: NDMO (2017). Members of CDDCCGs are appointed in line with the guidelines set out by the MoCC. The membership is determined based on the community's composition and is based on voluntary self-nomination followed by a community vote. Under the Handbook guidelines, each CDDCC must include the following representatives: a community leader,

## Vanuatu’s climate and recent trends

Vanuatu has a tropical maritime hot, humid, tropical climate, with year-round rainfall. There are two main seasons: a warm wet season, influenced by the northeast monsoon, between November and April; and a cooler dry season, influenced by the southeast trade winds, from May to October.

### 14. Meteorological data

Vanuatu has a long-term record of climate monitoring maintained by the Meteorology and Geo-Hazards Department. There are, however, gaps in the historical data record as a result of ongoing challenges with digitizing the paper record, the death of monitoring stations to capture current trends, and the geographical isolation of some islands.

#### Climate warming trend

The climate warming trend is clear and ongoing, and relates to an early baseline of 1850–1900. Vanuatu has experienced around +0.3°C warming compared to the 1986-2005 period.

#### Sea surface temperature (SST)

Sea surface temperature (SST) varies seasonally and has been observed to be increasing over recent decades, with a rate of increase of approximately 0.4 °C per decade since 1973. SST is an important factor that can influence the location of primary productivity zones and availability of nutrients in the ocean. It also influences the intensity and frequency of tropical cyclones, ocean salinity, and the overall health of marine ecosystems. Increase in SST has also been observed to be associated with a decrease in cloud cover over Vanuatu, with an average rate of 0.4 °C per decade[1]. There is little spatial variation in Vanuatu in the annual trend. This annual trend is consistent across all seasons.

#### Seasonal variations

There are no clear changes in seasonal or annual rainfall over Vanuatu since 1907. Over this period, there have been substantial variations in rainfall from year to year but little change in extreme daily rainfall (≥50 mm).

#### Tropical cyclones

Tropical cyclones (TC) most commonly affect Vanuatu during the warm wet season, between November and April. The number of TCs that have affected Vanuatu has increased over time. In 2016, two TCs passed into the Vanuatu Exclusive Economic Zone, or an average of 24 cyclones per decade. The number of cyclones varies from one year to another with some years out to six in others. Over this period, cyclones occurred in El Niño, La Niña and neutral phases.

Vanuatu has experienced two category 5 cyclones (TC Pam in 2015 and TC Harold in 2020) with estimated economic losses of approximately $34 million and $87 million respectively. The most recent was TC Harold:

- Wind and waves around Vanuatu do not vary significantly throughout the year, having constant wave heights and periods.
- Waves are influenced by the southern trade winds and movement of the South Equatorial Current (SEC) in the summer months and the Southern Annual Mode. Waves come mainly from the south-east, consisting of wind-generated waves and a component of swell propagated from storm events in the Southern Ocean. Wave height is highest during the wet season.

## Climate Change Projections for Vanuatu

Climate change projections for Vanuatu have been delivered by the Pacific Climate Change Science Program (PCCSP) and the Pacific-Australia Climate Change Science and Adaptation Planning program (PACCASAP). The PCCSP and PACCASAP modeling is based on climate model simulations following a set of emission scenarios. These models are used to project future changes in temperature, rainfall, ocean chemistry, sea level rise, and other variables for the entire Pacific region as well as specific areas such as the Vanuatu region of the western tropical Pacific. These 24 models have been used to develop climate projections for Vanuatu.

In 2018, CSIRO commercial the process of updating the PCCSP and PACCASAP projections (under the NextGen project), utilizing the same underlying data. The updated projections confirm the findings of the PCCSP and PACCASAP models that Vanuatu will experience significant climate change impacts.

The AR6 report provides a more detailed understanding of three key climate drivers in Vanuatu: extreme rainfall, tropical cyclones and sea level rise. These are discussed further below under the Sixth Assessment Report (AR6). In particular, trends in temperature, rainfall, ocean chemistry, sea level rise and other variables for the entire Pacific region as well as specific areas such as the Vanuatu region of the western tropical Pacific.

The two lowest emissions scenarios, RCP2.6 and RCP4.5, are below the current global emissions trajectory. According to AR5, in 2011 the total radiative forcing relative to 1750 reached 2.92 W/m² with high confidence (95%). This is expected to increase further by 2030 and beyond due to the increase in GHG concentrations in the atmosphere. Given this trend, RCP2.6 is unlikely to be achieved unless significant emissions reductions are made. The remaining scenarios under the Paris Agreement, RCP4.5 is achievable if all current pledges are met and the remaining RCPs to be updated commensurately increase ambition and that ambition continues to increase over time.

As a nation highly vulnerable to climate change impacts, Vanuatu needs to protect its population from these changes in order to achieve its long-term development goals (represented by RCP0), rather than an aspirational one (represented by RCP2.6) or even that which is possible with current commitments (RCP4.5).

This is particularly the case as there remain significant uncertainties at the level of policy response and implementation, including the choice of pathway to achieve the Paris Agreement goals (the implementation framework or pathway to achievement). Therefore, the impact assessment for this project is based on a range of emissions scenarios, from low emissions scenario (RCP4.5), which is recommended for the planet to avoid significant ecological tipping points, and the very high emissions scenario (RCP8.5). There are two further considerations that may be relevant in relation to the RCPs:

- The projected changes do not diverge in a significant way between the RCPs until around 2040.
- The direction of change for all emissions scenarios is the same (e.g., higher temperatures, more concentrated rainfall), with increasing intensity depending on longer term trajectories. Application of the precautionary principle, as a matter of good governance, would then point to the use of a higher-rCP.

22. Considerations above and the availability of scientific data on climate change forecasts and impacts specific to Vanuatu, the project design is informed primarily by the medium emissions scenario (RCP4.5) for temperature, precipitation, sea level rise and extreme events.

The project activities are also designed to be re-ignitable adaptation actions – they will help increase resilience in the face of significant impacts, while still making sense in lower emissions scenarios, and even in a world without climate change, as they will reduce vulnerability to other hazards such as cyclones and natural disasters extremes.


### Greenhouse gas emission scenarios (RCP6.0) for future climate conditions can be summarized as follows:

* Increases in daily air temperatures are projected across all of Vanuatu for minimum, mean and maximum temperature levels. Compared to 1986-2005, the increase will be higher by 0.3°C by 2050, and 1.3-2°C by 2090 (high confidence);
* Extreme daily maximum temperatures at all levels and become more frequent. By 2040, the current day maximum occurring once every 20 years will occur every 2 years (high confidence);
* Increases in sea surface temperatures will mean reefs around Vanuatu will experience conditions that exceed their thermal tolerance limits for up to 15% of the time by 2030 and 25°C more often, but impacts will be spatially and temporally variable (high confidence);
* Extreme rainfall events are projected to increase in frequency and intensity (high confidence). By 2040, the 1-in-100 year event intensity will increase to 10-11%. This is the same as across all islands;
* Frequencies of current extreme events will increase by 1.2-5 times.
* The duration of extreme rainfall events are projected to become longer (low confidence). The 1 in 5-year event will likely lengthen from 9 days to 20 days.


### Tropical cyclones are projected to be less frequent (decrease in cyclone formation) but more intense and destructive.

### Sea level is estimated to be currently increasing by 6 mm/year since 1990. Models simulate an increase of sea level rise between 15-35 cm by 2050, with increases of up to 80 cm (30-80 cm) indicated by 2090 (high confidence).


### In 20 years' time, it is projected that continued ocean acidification will result in seawater chemistry that will be unsuitable for the survival and/or structure and accretion on 60% of coral reefs around the world, including those in Vanuatu.


24. The current climate change impacts are expected to increase the challenges facing communities in Vanuatu as they face increasing frequency and/or intensity of extreme events:

* Temperatures: air and sea (minimum, mean and maximum daily temperatures and events such as heatwaves);
* Rainfall patterns (increased duration of dry periods, changing frequency and intensity of extreme rainfall and ENSO-connected rainfall);
* Tropical cyclones (increasing intensity, not frequency, predicted including several wind and waves, intense rainfall and flooding).

## Temperature

25. Projections for all emissions pathways indicate that the annual average air temperature and SST will increase in the future in Vanuatu, and the magnitude of projected change is similar for the next 20 years under any plausible emissions pathway, but after 2040 there is growing and notable differences between projections from different emission pathways. Under a high emissions scenario (RCP8.5), this increase in temperature is projected to be in the range of 0.6–1.7°C, while under a low emissions scenario (RCP2.6) it will be less than 0.3°C.

26. In Vanuatu, warming has occurred and is projected to continue occurring at a lower rate than the global average over this century. The observed temperature increase in Vanuatu is consistent with that of the ocean. Step-like changes in air temperature have occurred in the past and are likely to happen in the future, and therefore planned adaptation actions should consider non-linear and unpredictable warming in the future. There is a high level of confidence (90% or greater) for the observed warming trend up to date) and very high confidence that this trend will continue for the foreseeable future regardless of the emissions scenario.

27. The uncertainty around rainfall projections for Vanuatu as model outputs are not consistent. Some models suggest a slight increase in wet season rainfall and a decrease in dry season rainfall, however the overall projection is for little change. Wet and dry years will still occur in response to natural variability, but extreme events (e.g., prolonged droughts or heavy downpours) are expected to occur more often. Drought frequency is expected to decrease slightly by the end of the century.

28. Projections indicate that rainfall changes over Vanuatu are likely to be less significant than temperature change, and trends are less obvious given the very high climate variability. The average rainfall in Vanuatu has been increasing since 1970 (Figure 3), but this is not a reliable indicator of possible future trajectories, from wetter through to drier, largely determined by future change in the South Pacific Convergence Zone. Therefore, planned adaptation actions should consider the impacts of both increased and decreased rainfall on water resources and ecosystems (including potential implications for rain-fed agriculture).

| Figure 3 | Historical and simulated surface air temperature time series for the region surrounding Vanuatu. The graph shows the anomaly (from 1960–2005) in monthly mean surface air temperature from observations (black), the smoothed 20-year running mean of the observations (blue), and the ensemble mean of the historical simulations (red). The shaded area represents the range of the very high RCP8.5, in red) and very low RCP2.6 (in blue) emissions scenarios. The solid red and blue lines show the smoothed 20-year running mean of the historical simulations for RCP8.5 and RCP2.6 respectively. The black line shows the observed temperature anomaly from 1970 to present day. The dashed lines show the 50th percentile of the observed interannual variability (in black) and added to the projections as a range of possible future trajectories, from wetter through to drier, largely determined by future change in the South Pacific Convergence Zone.

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 11

29 While the annual quantum of rainfall may not change dramatically—or at least it isn't currently possible to reliably project the direction of any change, people in Vanuatu will continue to live with significant variability in their rainfall and water availability. The climate model projections indicate that climate change will increase the variability of the country's rainfall: This is especially true for the remote and rural populations the VCCRP will target, given their reliance on subsistence rain-fed agriculture, which is particularly vulnerable to changes in rainfall patterns. Climate change is expected to lead to more intense storms leading to flooding, droughts, heatwaves, salinization, increases in evapotranspiration, seasonal variability of precipitation and increased frequency of extreme weather events. The amount of water expected to decrease significantly, and potential evapotranspiration is increased, indicating significant stress on non-irrigated agriculture. This will lead to a reduction in the area under cultivation and an increase in loss of soil fertility due to deforestation and environmental degradation. In order to ensure vulnerable remote communities are resilient to climate change impacts, the VCCRP proposes to develop and deliver adaptation actions in the agriculture sector that will help increase food security across a range of climate futures—from wetter to drier. The VCCRP activities will seek to do this.

#### Tropical cyclones

30 On a global scale, climate projections indicate there is likely to be a decrease in the number of tropical cyclones by the end of the 21st century. However, for cyclones that do occur, there is likely to be an increase in their intensity and duration. The projected change in frequency of TCs is about -30% (about +40%) within 100 km of the cyclone centre, and a likely (+around 15%) increase in the proportion of cyclones that reach category 4 or higher. There are no significant changes to the projected change in TC activity reported in AR5: Negligible change in overall frequency. Significant (>15%) increase in number of TCs reaching category 3 or above is unlikely, but there is a high confidence (about 70%) that compared to pre-industrial conditions, that there is 'high confidence' in a projected increase of TC rain rates at all intensities. The projected change in the proportion of TCs with maximum wind speeds greater than about 140 km/h is about +30%, and that there is 'high confidence for an increase in the proportion of TCs that reach the strongest (Category 4+ levels). The median projected increase in this proportion is about 13%.

## With regard to the implications of future projected climate change on tropical cyclones, the Noulis projections conclude that "the evidence suggests that for the southwestern Pacific as a whole, the total number of tropical cyclones is likely to increase in the near century, but with an increase in the average intensity and an increase in the length of tropical cyclone seasons, and greater coastal inundation due to sea level rise."

### Anticipated changes include:
- A projected increase of 2.8 cycles (ranging from 0-6 per year in recent decades).
- 29 of 71 tropical cyclones (41%) that have affected Vanuatu since 1981 have been severe.
- The frequency and intensity of extreme weather events are expected to increase, potentially causing catastrophic loss to lives and livelihoods of people, particularly in remote and rural coastal communities.

#### Additional factors:
- About 80% of the population being dependent on subsistence agriculture, climate change poses a significant risk to Vanuatu's agricultural sector and food security.
- Agricultural activities in Vanuatu are particularly vulnerable to climate change impacts such as increased frequency and intensity of droughts, flooding, heatwaves, salinization processes, increases in evapotranspiration, seasonal variations in rainfall patterns, and changes in temperature regimes.

#### Climate change will:
- Increase the risk of crop failure.
- Reduce yields for staple crops like yams and taro.
- Decrease water availability and increase soil erosion.
- Lead to increased salinity levels in coastal areas.
- Cause more frequent and intense storms, damaging seedlings and encouraging conditions that permit weeds to flourish.

#### Prolonged and intense rainfall:

## Additional burden from climate change: disaster risk reduction
37. Climate change adaptation (CCA) and disaster risk reduction (DRR) articles share the aim of building the resilience of people, economies, and natural resources to the impacts of climate change. Access to Community-based Early Warning Systems (EWS) for disaster risk are more widespread, but still sporadic. Key opportunities to enhance DRR at the community level include: strengthening local capacity in early warning systems; incorporating potential future climate change impacts into local planning and budgeting decisions; strengthening local level CCA and DRR measures through participatory approaches that involve communities in decision-making processes in local language and supporting socially-inclusive community groups focused on local DRR and CCA.

## Research shows that reduced food security is a key factor in driving increased rural and agrarian poverty. This in turn can trigger an increased dependence on global supply chains and aid. Initially in rural areas, this may lead to increased migration but also flows through to urban markets. Lack of food security from climate impacts on the food supply chain will increase vulnerability to shocks and stresses over the long-term, will reduce the resilience of the population following climate-related disasters and increase the occurrence of conflict. The lack of food security in rural areas, in particular, may also trigger broader societal changes such as forced migration, crime and disease.

### B (2) a. Theory of change narrative and diagram (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagram)

The theory of change is based on the Vanuatu Climate Resilience Framework. The Framework provides an overarching structure that focuses on meeting eight pre-conditions for climate-resilience and supports their interlinking and mutually reinforcing.

All community members have their basic needs met,
1. Access to a diverse, climate-resilient set of livelihoods strategies.
2. Ability to participate in and influence decision-making processes.
3. Access to, and ability to utilize, traditional and scientific climate information.
4. Information on the impacts of increasing climate variability, extremes and change on their food security and livelihoods.
5. Increased understanding of the changing context of shocks and stresses, and ability to take action to reduce vulnerability.
6. Strong in community and external networks to share and draw on knowledge, resources and ideas.
7. Connected government at all levels that listens and is responsive to community needs, is innovative, and has strong leadership that is transparent and accountable.

The Vanuatu Climate Resilience Framework builds upon the Vanuatu Climate Resilience Framework, developed under a previous community-based adaptation project. This in turn was a modified version of the Climate Change Resilience Framework (CCRF) developed by the Australian academic partners under the Africa Climate Change Resilience Alliance program.

The components of the framework are interlinked and mutually reinforcing. The eight pre-conditions for climate-resilience are all conditionally interlinked and mutually reinforcing. The VCORP will work to help ensure these pre-conditions are met as part of its approach to building community resilience via the adaptation project. This is achieved through three components (Component 1), (Component 2) and (Component 3).

### B (2) b. Project theory of change statement

The project seeks to address that vulnerable and remote rural communities in Vanuatu access and utilize locally relevant climate information, integrate climate risks into community planning processes, and are better able to adapt their livelihoods strategies to reduce the impacts of increasing climate variability, extremes and change on their food security and livelihoods. Because their exposure and vulnerability to hazards will be reduced and their adaptive capacity increased.

### B (2) c. Core problem statement

The core climate problem the project seeks to address is that remote and rural communities in Vanuatu suffer from limited access to locally relevant climate information, poor planning processes, and insecure access to key services undermining resilience. To address these deficits, the project will work to build local community capacity related to food security and livelihoods, based on a Foundation of increased knowledge and understanding of climate change impacts, improved community health.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 16

## B

### Adaptation Planning and Enhanced National and Sub-National Government Capacity to Support Climate Actions

The project utilizes the IPCC's definition of adaptive capacity, as articulated in the Third Assessment Report (TAR), which refers to the potential or ability of a system, region, or community to adapt to the effects or impacts of climate change. Enhancement of adaptive capacity represents a practical means of reducing vulnerability and increasing resilience.

### Core Project Focus

The core project is focused on the adaptation packages under Component 2. The package is a suite of adaptive activities that address the main drivers of community vulnerability. These activities were identified through a national vulnerability assessment and stakeholder engagement process, and are aligned with the National Adaptation Plan (NAP) and the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). The project will support communities in implementing these adaptation packages.

### Participatory Stakeholder Involvement

Participatory stakeholder involvement during implementation will allow for a tailored set of adaptation actions to be selected from the adaptation package, enhancing local ownership and promoting long-term sustainability. This approach ensures that all stakeholders have an opportunity to participate in, and lead, all stages of adaptation action, from selection to implementation, along with capacity building and monitoring and evaluation.

### Adaptation Activities

Adaptation activities have been developed considering structural challenges experienced by women and youth, particularly in the areas of water resources, agriculture, and fisheries for food security outputs can be scaled up as needed to suit local context. The project will work through a network of community-based organizations where available to maximize the appropriateness, ownership, and sustainability of adaptation actions.

### Project Approach

This approach will ensure Vanuatu can shift from a fragmented (non-standard) approach in some locations, to a coordinated spatially inclusive and decentralized approach at all levels (government and communities). The project will target Local Area Councils and apply an uptake scorecard to measure the level of effectiveness of adopted adaptation actions.

### Barriers and Costs

Vanuatu faces many barriers that prevent the country from adequately addressing climate impacts with limited resources. A recent study by the World Bank (2019) shows that the costs of overall infrastructure and adaptation activities is a significant proportion of GDP, and well beyond the financial means of a developing economy (especially a SIDS). The project will work to address these barriers through capacity building and institutional strengthening, further enhanced by external factors such as fluctuating labor markets and population growth.

### Key Components

Key components for achieving locally-led adaptation in Vanuatu identified during the project's design process include:

- Institutional capacity gaps at local, sub-national, and national levels which hamper effective adaptation planning processes and implementation of adaptation actions.

## External adaptation deficits (high exposure to frequent hazards and insecure access to services) impede communities' ability to engage in activities and reduce project impact; and Low incentives for private sector entities to engage with remote rural communities to support increased food production.

### These socio-cultural-institutional barriers interact with the key climate change risks identified for targeted communities, which include direct biophysical impacts associated with climate changes and Food security and livelihoods. Ongoing support from government and an increased local ability to

## Combined, these risks and barriers are undermining the viability of traditional livelihoods and food security in highly climate vulnerable communities. This is particularly the case for the ecosystems and ecosystems services that underpin the livelihoods of many communities in Vanuatu. The project will address these socio-cultural-institutional barriers by building on existing community structures (GCF Outcome Area 4) as a critical component of the project. For the targeted communities, this will be achieved through the development of an integrated approach to climate change adaptation that is intricately interwoven with resilient and functioning ecosystems.

### Vanuatu has a long history of adapting to environmental changes in ocean conditions and hold strong traditional knowledge. While traditional knowledge will be valuable in dealing with the longer-term ecological impacts of climate change, it cannot provide all the information needed for effective use of contemporary science and knowledge. While there is credible scientific information available about climate change in Vanuatu, applying the information to decision-making for sectors is not straightforward because of a lack of understanding of how to interpret this information, limitations such as technological limitations and isolation, and also in terms of education to enable this information to be expressed in ways that are understandable by communities. This can lead to misinterpretation or even misinterpreted by the public during decision-making, if they are used at all.

### Targeted communities will be supported through a participatory approach to climate change adaptation. The Government of Vanuatu’s 2019-2030 NAPSP Baseline Survey on food security found 30% of the rural population (65,330 people) report that they are food insecure and 48% of these households have experienced at least one episode of repeated use of agricultural plots (shortened fallow times), droughts and pests. Communities report that 4-12 months are typically required to recover subsistence agriculture after a typical cyclone. The risks of food insecurity in Vanuatu are likely to increase as climate change continues to impact the region, with rapid increase in population and increased reliance on imported and purchased (rather than locally produced) food. This will be further exacerbated by increasing competition for land (particularly cash) incomes of rural Vanuatu. Project activities will help reduce communities’ experience of hunger through a range of interventions that include: improving access to markets; strengthening local seed preservation and processing for storage (reducing food losses) and markets. Household incomes in rural and urban areas are also highly reliant on food production, with income mainly sourced from the sale of primary produce.

### The project will help shift communities from a purely traditional knowledge-based approach to resource management towards an integrated approach that is informed by both local and scientific knowledge and decision-making. The project offers adaptation solutions building on local knowledge, skills and innovations in order to improve food security and livelihoods for vulnerable communities in Vanuatu. Food security and livelihoods in Vanuatu – fisheries and agriculture. Vulnerable communities and ecosystems are particularly at risk from climate change impacts such as cyclones, droughts and pests. The project will focus on the development of an integrated approach that is informed by both local and scientific knowledge and decision-making to improve food security and livelihoods. ## Immediate benefits will be achieved through community-level actions that address priority adaptation needs (including via increasing food security and diversifying livelihood options), with a focus on addressing the most vulnerable communities, particularly women, youth, and persons with disabilities (social inclusion and geography), increasing access to relevant and useable climate information and building adaptive capacity at local level. Medium-term benefits will be achieved through the participation and empowerment of communities in decision-making processes that are informed by climate science and evidence of climate impacts. Medium-term benefits will be achieved through the participation and empowerment of communities in decision-making processes that are informed by climate science and evidence of climate impacts, effects, improving access to, and ability to use, climate information and manage knowledge, and strengthening local government systems and institutions. Long-term benefits will be achieved through increased resilience to anticipated climate change impacts. Long-term benefits will be achieved by working at the sub-local, national and regional levels to increase adaptive capacity and the ability to make forward-looking decisions in response to climate change. The project’s theory of change is summarized in Figure 4 below, before going on to describe how the project will achieve its objectives and results. For more detail see, at the end of Section B.3.

## As noted above, ACCIP will build on a range of previous adaptation actions implemented in:

- *Villages*: scaling up existing approaches to building climate resilient food security in remote and rural community and implementing innovative (for the context) approaches to food preservation and processing, including the use of solar drying technology. The project will also seek to build on the achievements of GCF FP035 - scaling out sector-relevant climate information services development and dissemination at sub-national level.

- *District*: building capacity in 200 communities, creating a paradigm shift in climate-related decision-making at the local level.

## Its phased approach is expected to result in increases across a range of economic, social, environmental and gender co-benefits, including:

- Increasing the household incomes of rural communities through livelihoods diversification and increased access to markets (co-benefit 1);
- Increasing broad community health outcomes by ensuring communities have more secure access to locally-available nutritious foods (co-benefit 2);
- Increasing the number of days that children attend school each day in the aftermath of extreme weather events through adaptive planning and enhanced DRR action (co-benefit 3);
- Increasing the sustainability of ecosystem services by reducing reliance on vulnerable resources (e.g. a reduction in the use of firewood) (co-benefit 4);
- Increasing gender equality across all areas of community life – with a specific focus on ensuring women’s voices are heard in climate-related decision-making forums and that the gendered nature of climate change is considered in planning, implementation and development planning at community and province levels (co-benefit 5);
- Increasing social inclusion across all areas of community life – with a specific focus on ensuring voices from vulnerable groups such as women, youth, and persons with disabilities are heard in climate-related decision-making forums (co-benefit 6).

## For the project’s theory of change to be realized, a number of assumptions need to hold. These include:

- Multiple livelihoods will be maintained by households affected by significant climate-related disasters (category 5: cyclone, wave action, flood, drought, heatwave, etc.) during implementation and prior to key project activities being implemented.

# B.2 (b). Outcome mapping to GCF results area and co-benefit categorization

## B.3. Project programme description (max. 2500 words, approximately 5 pages)

The project is designed to minimize climate change vulnerability and maximize resilience among communities in Vanuatu by implementing a range of adaptation actions. The project is based on a solid understanding of community vulnerability to climate change and existing adaptive capacity derived from existing knowledge and experience. It will also build the adaptive capacity of communities to reduce relative climate vulnerability of all Area Councils nation-wide. The national vulnerability assessment was conducted in 2013, which identified the main drivers (or sources) of vulnerability for each area council to hazards, food and livelihood systems and knowledge on which community-level actors increase sensitivity and/or undermine adaptive capacity. Stakeholder consultation and field missions validated the results of the national assessment and provided additional information about existing adaptive capacity (method details available in Annex 2) consistent with the National Vulnerability Assessment Framework.

The results provide a relative ranking of all Area Councils in Vanuatu from highest to lowest vulnerability, which is used as an input for prioritizing adaptation actions that address the main drivers (or sources) of vulnerability, which were used to identify a suite of adaptation actions that specifically address the main sources of vulnerability and provide a menu or packages of adaptations for implementation by Area Councils.

B

from their own (adaptation package) and implemented by beneficiary communities with support from government ministries and other procured parties to minimize vulnerability and build resilience to climate change impacts. The map below shows the distribution of various types of vulnerable areas: high and low vulnerable Area Councils exist on the same island and in each Province (see Figure 5 – a larger version of this map is at Annex 16), demonstrating the influence of local conditions (topographical and socio-economic) on vulnerability.

The relationship of the components to each other and the overall approach for implementing them is summarized in Annex 23. The methodology used for assessing vulnerability and determining community targeting is annexed in Annex 2 (Section 5.3) and Annex 23.

The vulnerability assessment process drew on available data for climate and non-climate hazards, and vulnerability indicators were developed based on this information and displayed in Table 18. Stakeholder consultations provided important data for this process. Using this knowledge, the assessment focused on:

- hazard (climate and non-climate hazards);
- exposure (shoreline geomorphology, topography);
- sensitivity (population density and age structure, access to food, dependence of crops for food, dependence of livestock for income, condition of habitats, remoteness / accessibility); and
- adaptability (access to health services, education levels, health index).

Notably, indicators of climate change vulnerability are not only related to climate but also socio-ecological factors that influence the intensity of impacts from climate change and their ability to adapt to future risks. Indicators with a high risk level (medium or high) were developed for each indicator. A panel of 12 technical specialists who assigned scores for each indicator.

The findings of the vulnerability assessment were then tested in a sample of communities across six provinces in which the project will work, reflecting the reality of challenges facing remote and vulnerable communities.

The low vulnerability rankings for each Area Council were then put through a participatory process involving recent, current and planned adaptation projects, programmes and activities, including planning / delivering activities in each Area Council. This project will work with 282 communities across all six provinces in which the project will work, reaching 382 communities and directly assisting 105,647 people. These communities are considered direct beneficiaries because the scale of activities will increase the climate resilience of all women, girls and boys in each targeted community (see Table 1, below). The full analysis and all data are available in Annex 23.

## Adaptation Package - approach and content

74. The core intervention strategy of the project is delivery of the adaptation packages under Component 2. These are designed to address the drivers of climate change vulnerability (typically identified through a national vulnerability assessment and aligned with the resilience framework) in each community. Each package will be tailored to meet the specific needs of a specific adaptation package gives communities options to select the most appropriate and effective adaptation actions for their situation. The adaptation packages are designed to address the core vulnerability specific to the needs of each individual community. Activities in the adaptation package are designed to be implemented by the communities themselves, with technical assistance provided as required depending on community circumstances. This makes the package approach tailored and highly transferable to other neighboring communities in Vanuatu as well as other Pacific Island countries.

75. Participatory stakeholder engagement is a key element of this project. It will allow for a tailored set of adaptation actions to be selected from the adaptation package and increased local ownership, promoting long-term sustainability of the actions. The project will provide technical assistance to communities in order to participate in and lead stages of adaptation, from selection to implementation to operation and maintenance. This will ensure that the community is able to manage the project on a day-to-day basis by all beneficiaries and help the sustainability of the implemented actions. Final approval of

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 24

## Community Adaptation Plans, which include the community’s specific adaptation package, will sit with the MiCC, as co-Exceeding Entity link to national climate change policy processes.

The adaptation actions are designed to be implemented over the six-year project and have flexibility to accommodate changes in the context of climate change. The adaptation actions will be selected from a menu of options that includes components of natural resource management and climate resilient agriculture and fisheries for food security and livelihoods (Output 2.1), climate-resilient small-scale agriculture for food security and livelihoods (Output 2.2), climate-resilient artisanal fisheries for food security and livelihoods (Output 2.3), and climate-resilient processing and preservation to support food security and diversification of livelihoods (Output 2.4).

Components of natural resource management and climate resilient agriculture and fisheries for food security and livelihoods will be part of the community’s adaptation package, which will form part of their broader Community Adaptation Plan (the development pathway). The project will work with communities to develop a menu of actions that each community can select from in order to adapt to climate change. Each community will then select a number of actions from the adaptation package menu that respond to the needs identified in their Community Vulnerability and Climate Change Impact Profile (CVCCIP) or beyond the scope of this document, which will be part of each community’s adaptation package. The Community Adaptation Plan may contain activities that go beyond the remit of the CVCCIP. In these cases, the project will support communities to seek other sources of funding for implementation and/or to include them as part of the development pathway of the Community Adaptation Plan. The project-supported adaptation packages and the investment plan will both form part of the community’s overall adaptation strategy.

Adaptation activities have been developed considering structural disadvantages experienced by women, youth, and people with disabilities in accessing resources and services. The project development pathways also promote social justice and equality in decision-making. The project will work through a number of local organizations to ensure that these groups are included as key stakeholders or organizations where available to maximize the appropriateness, ownership and sustainability of adaptation actions.

The Adaptation package is made up of a menu of actions that fall under four broad community sectors:

- Increasing the resilience of local natural resources upon which the local communities depend to climate change (Output 2.1)
- Climate-resilient small-scale agriculture for food security and livelihoods (Output 2.2)
- Climate-resilient artisanal fisheries for food security and livelihoods (Output 2.3)
- Climate-resilient processing and preservation to support food security and diversification of livelihoods (Output 2.4)

The overarching package is outlined below in Figure 7, including the linkages between actions. Specific activities for each component of the package are provided in the section below.

## Components, outputs and activities

### Component 1: Government, civil society and communities are strengthened to support local resilience to climate change impacts, including by providing access to climate information and early warning systems.

The resilience of rural communities hinges largely on their awareness of climate change impacts, preparedness for these changes, and the ability to adapt. This component builds capacity at the community level on reliable early warning systems; and to make long term development decisions based on participatory risk assessments. The Community-based Climate Change Adaptation and Disaster Risk Reduction Component builds community and institutional capacity at the local level, integrated into a provincial structure, to plan for climate change impacts through an adaptive management approach.

### Output 1: Community-based climate change adaptation and disaster risk reduction mechanisms are strengthened

Climate change impacts will result in an increase of disaster events which will have significant and ongoing impacts on communities. Strengthening local-level DRR mechanisms (the CCDCCS) is a critical component of reducing risk.
Activities to be implemented include:
- CCDCCCS established and strengthened in at least 254 communities (assumed at 90% of targeted communities)

## Activity descriptions

### 1.1 Establish Community Disaster and Climate Change Committees (CDCCCs) if necessary and build their capacity.

#### 1.1.1 Establish new, or strengthen existing CDCCCs and build their capacity, including strengthening social inclusion and gender balance.
- Support will be provided to the national government (regional governments in some cases) to establish new CDCCCs where they do not exist, or to strengthen existing ones.
- CDCCC assessment instrument, CDCCC assessment indicators and methodology will be developed through a participatory process with stakeholders. The assessment tool will be based on a ground-truthed analysis of local contexts of where CDCCCs have been established, which are currently operating in the country.
- A pilot phase will take place to evaluate and improve the instrument prior to full implementation with all target sites.
- Considerations for strengthening existing CDCCCs include: how to strengthen their capacity (such as including children/youth with disability), on how to strengthen their CDCCCs (such as improving gender balance). Based on CDCCC assessment instrument findings, tailored support will be provided to substantiate the needs of each site. This will include training and technical assistance for the development of CDCCCs, including, where needed, CDCCC kits (containing two smart phones, one tablet, a projector, a flip chart, and other materials) that can be used by the CDCCCs in their work.

#### 1.1.2 Increase CDCCC member capacity to identify climate change and disaster risks at the local level, including providing leadership training (targeted at women) and establishing communication channels for CDCCC members to share information on climate change and disaster risk.
- Activity 1.1.2 will increase the capacity of CDCCC members in effectively identifying climate and disaster risk within their respective geographic boundaries. Based on CDCCC assessment findings, traditional community education sessions will be provided (targeted at women) or leadership training (adaptation and disaster risk reduction) sessions aligned to their respective needs and strengths.
- Traditional community education sessions will be provided where the capacity of CDCCC members is low in leadership-oriented training to enable greater participation in local adaptation planning processes. Follow-up support will be provided as needed, including additional training on climate change and disaster risks as needs arise. CDCCCs will be supported in establishing new, or strengthening existing communication channels for CDCCC members to share information on climate change and disaster risk.
- Considerations for strengthening existing CDCCCs include: how to strengthen their capacity (such as improving gender balance). Based on CDCCC assessment instrument findings, tailored support will be provided to substantiate the needs of each site. This will include training and technical assistance for the development of CDCCCs, including, where needed, CDCCC kits (containing two smart phones, one tablet, a projector, a flip chart, and other materials) that can be used by the CDCCCs in their work.

### 1.2 Communities have increased understanding of climate change impacts and are supported to develop inclusive local adaptation plans.
- In order to take effective adaptation action, communities require an increased understanding of the implications of climate change on their livelihoods, including food security, water sources and livelihoods and the ability to effectively plan for current and future adaptation needs.

### 1.3 CDCCCs have increased capacity to deliver awareness raising materials on climate change and disaster risks to food systems, livelihoods and disaster risk.

- 148,267 people have increased the capacity to deliver awareness raising materials on climate change and disaster risks to food systems, livelihoods and disaster risk
- 144,390 people have increased their awareness/post on climate change risks and impacts delivered to public via media/social media.

### Activity descriptions

#### 1.2.1: Community awareness raising on climate risk to food systems, livelihoods and disaster risk with information, education and communications (IEC) materials and information sessions managed by Area Council Climate Change Officers and CDDCs
* **Activity 1.2.1a:** Develop and disseminate IEC materials for communities on climate change and disaster risks to food systems and livelihoods by supporting Area Councils and CDDCs with tailored IEC materials, including those developed under the project (e.g., GIZ, RECCUSE, PEBAC) as well as existing climate change education materials (including those developed by other projects including GIZ, RECCUSE, PEBAC). See Annex 2. Aimed at community-level audiences will be reviewed to identify key messages and develop tailored IEC materials. The materials will be disseminated to MoCC and MOCs to incorporate project level information on climate change risks to food systems and livelihoods in national climate change education programs. Existing IEC materials will be revised, existing material being developed for teaching and capacity building purposes. Digitized train-the-trainer sessions will be conducted with Area Council Climate Change Officers and CDDCs on use of newly developed digital and paper-based IEC materials and key messages.
* **Activity 1.2.1b:** Disseminate IEC materials to communities, including through Area Councils and organizations, and other local stakeholders. In conjunction with Activity 3.2.2, in-person knowledge exchange sessions will be organized for Area Council Climate Change Officers, CDDCs, national, and local stakeholders to facilitate an additional entry point to consideration of local issues in subnational / national climate change adaptation planning processes.

#### 1.2.2: Identify key local issues that drive climate vulnerability and develop local adaptation plans and action programs based on biophysical and ecological survey of key natural resources (habitats and species) that support food security and livelihoods as well as household socioeconomic status, including documentation of local natural resource use and existing local adaptation actions
* **Activity 1.2.2a:** Identification of key changes and trends that affect climate directly as well as existing local resources and adaptive capacities to inform development of local adaptation plans. The focus will be on the identification of key changes and trends affecting CDDC members in facilitating community profiling and assessing / documenting local climate vulnerabilities in alignment with the National Vulnerability Assessment Framework (NVAF) and other national sector guidelines. Community profiling and vulnerability assessments will be undertaken by Area Council Climate Change Officers, CDDCs, and other stakeholders.
* **Activity 1.2.2b:** Participatory community profiling and local vulnerability assessments will be undertaken by Local Area Councils with support from CDDCs to identify key changes and trends affecting local food systems and livelihoods will be carried out, including assessment of threats to water resources, soil fertility, crop yields, livestock productivity, and agricultural pests and diseases. In addition to collecting biophysical and ecological base data for assessing climate risks and vulnerabilities, the profiling process will also collect baseline data (such as VNSD) to assess local natural resource dependence to inform adaptation interventions.
* **Activity 1.2.2c:** Development of inclusive Community Adaptation Plans and identification of key resilience building actions (selected from adaptation package menu) through participatory processes facilitated by sub-national government and CDDCs.

#### 1.2.3: Development of inclusive Community Adaptation Plans and identification of key resilience building actions (selected from adaptation package menu) through participatory processes facilitated by sub-national government and CDDCs

## Activity 1.2: Facilitate development of Community Adaptation Plans and identification of key climate resilience building actions from the VCORP adaptation package menu of interventions.

Building on findings from Activity 1.2.2, this activity will support the BUA, a participatory engagement process that can be undertaken at the community level to develop Adaptation Plans, helping prioritize immediate to mid-term adaptation actions to address current and projected climate change risks, as well as long-term priorities for VCORP adaptation interventions. The Community Adaptation Plan will provide a foundation from which communities identify their local preference and priorities for VCORP adaptation interventions that are most relevant to the community’s specific needs in terms of addressing environmental and social impacts, and address the underlying local drivers of climate change vulnerability. This activity will also support the development of a baseline assessment of access to EWS and CIS via the VCORP (adaptation packages) and longer-term priorities as well as identifying specific adaptation interventions that are most relevant for each community.

The Community Adaptation Plan is an integral component of the Community Adaptation Plan. Validation by community members (including Area Councils) will be used to enable prioritization of Community Adaptation Plans and prioritization of VCORP adaptation interventions at the community level. Community Adaptation Plans will then be updated on a three-year cycle.

Output 1.3 will build on GCF FP053 (Van-KIRAP) to disseminate usable, accessible climate information services and early warning systems (EWS) in communities such that the climate information generated under Van-KIRAP reaches the most vulnerable communities.

* **Activity 1.3.1:** Will support development and distribution of CIE IEC products to inform community-level climate change adaptation planning, including a baseline assessment of existing access to EWS and CIS in target communities will be undertaken, including translation into local languages where necessary. A needs assessment of access to EWS and CIS in target communities will be undertaken, including translation into local languages where necessary. The baseline assessment will be informed by a review of best practice on EWS. Based on demonstrated baseline assessment findings, support will be provided to enhance CCDCCC infrastructure through the installation of solar PV systems and internet connectivity with systems for on-set data selling for financial sustainability.

92 Activity 1.3.2 will strengthen the capacity of Area Councils and CDDCCOs in the use of CIS resources and tools for climate change adaptation planning at the local level. The activities are designed to prepare that address community needs and are tailored for community-level understanding.

### Component 2: Scalable, locally appropriate actions are implemented to meet community adaptation needs to create climate-resilient, sustainable development pathways

93 Vulnerability of communities is reduced by strengthening the resilience of natural resources and livelihoods if their primary production is made resilient to climate shocks. This component focuses on strengthening agriculture and fisheries systems to achieve food security and promoting economic activities through local value chains that are more resilient to climate change impacts.

The adaptation package menu includes a range of actions, which can be implemented at scale and is built around the adaptation package menu which is flexible and scalable to community contexts.

#### Output 2.1: Local natural resources are more resilient to climate change impacts through implementation of climate-resilient land use plans

94 Climate change impacts on natural resources that support food systems and livelihoods vary by location, and therefore require locally appropriate actions for adaptation. Actions under this sector will support communities to build the resilience of their local ecosystems to ensure it can continue to support food security and livelihoods as climate change impacts escalate.

- At least 254 land use plans developed to guide community decision-making around the allocation of common lands.
- 29 nurseries will be established to provide communities with native tree saplings to support reforestation.
- 10,000 seedlings and 500,000 cuttings of vetiver grass (Chrysopogon zizanioides) will be distributed to communities for reforestation, erosion control and shoreline protection.

As a result of the implementation of these actions, at least 21,684 hectares of land will be climate-resilient land management, and 29 erosion hotspots (one per Area Council) will be rehabilitated as fully rainfed agricultural areas.

- A total of 11,800 hectares of agricultural and fisheries sites under better management, including:
    - 500 hectares of upland forest areas along 29 Area Councils will be protected by extended tabu areas
    - 200 hectares of coastal area will be restored with native trees
    - 100 hectares of shorelines will be restored through establishing mangrove forests and invasive species management
- 250 hectares of critical watershed areas protected through installation of micro-check dams and erosion control (including establishing living barriers)
- 500 hectares of marine habitats will be protected by local conservation (tabu) areas

### Outcome 2.3: Climate-resilient fisheries for food security and livelihood development

**120 communities will be supported to adapt their fishing practices in climate-related threats such as marine heatwaves, events, cyclical and terrestrial runoff. As climate change impacts escalate especially in coastal areas, the project will support communities to better manage and maintain their fish populations and the catches needed to nourish households. Supporting communities to better manage and maintain their fish populations and catches will help reduce pressure on coastal reef ecosystems and supporting increased food security.**

### Activities

105 activities under this output will result in:

- Training provided to 2,436 people to effectively coastal resource management to help protect coastal and reef ecosystems from further damage (assumed 80% of households in the coastal project communities)
- Catch data recording system established in at least 202 communities to support more sustainable decision-making (assumed 10% targeted coastal communities)
- At least one community-level fisheries officer trained for local monitoring and management of coastal resources (assumed one female and one male champion in each of 95% of targeted coastal communities)
- 202 coastal communities establish community-led coastal resource management systems (assumed 80% of households in the coastal project communities)
- Off-reef fishing equipment distributed to 112 communities (assumes 50% targeted coastal communities)

### Activity description

**2.3.1: Build community capacity on coastal resource management and monitoring that supports sustainable fisheries, including va'a-based skills development**

105 activities under this activity will result in the establishment of coastal resource management through delivery of bespoke training and education programs. In parallel with Activity 2.1, communities will be supported to develop a local action plan for the management of their coastal resources (including mangrove clearing, overfishing, and current management and governance practices (including conservation or taboo areas)). Community leaders will identify community champions, including women, who will lead the development of the action plans and facilitate communication to team members. Coastal resource assessments will inform the development of culturally appropriate fisheries management strategies and will be shared with key sector stakeholders (including Vanuatu Fisheries Department (VFD) and community champions). The activity will also support the establishment of a 12-month cycle of coastal resource management training program, including pilot implementation prior to updating for further communities.

**2.3.2: Support communities to adopt primary-community-based fisheries management to reduce climate change impacts, including supporting community-level implementation of climate-resilient fisheries management strategies and practices that will help improve the resilience, productivity, diversification and sustainability of fisheries resources (alleviating pressures on inshore fisheries) by providing off-shore fishing equipment to communities**

105 activities under this activity will result in the establishment of primary-community-based fisheries management given small-scale fisheries with limited management and enforcement capacity. ## B

### Livelihood options to strengthen climate resilience. Local commodities that can be harvested or grown in target communities and are suitable for preservation will be promoted for cultivation and preservation. Preservation techniques such as drying, smoking, salting, pickling, freezing, and canning from agriculture and fisheries can be preserved. Target communities will be provided with educational information on relevant food processing and preservation techniques. Adaptation technologies for food production and processing will be introduced to target communities through training of trainers in communities to facilitate food processing. Target communities will be provided with support towards long-term adoption of adaptation technologies, including provision of seedlings, tools, equipment (e.g., replacement parts, refresher trainings, and upskilling as needed). Key stakeholders (including Area Councils) will be involved in the identification and addressing barriers to support successful adoption of adaptation technology.

#### 2.2: Support women to diversify into new agricultural/food commodities and value-add products that deliver income opportunities for women, including identifying local value-add commodities with market potential and supporting processing

1. Activity 2.4.2 will deliver outreach and educational information on recommended value-added production techniques and materials to individual target communities based on relevance. A set of targeted communities will be supported in the development of a business plan for value-added products (e.g., dried fruits, dried nuts, smoked fish, shell crabs, copra, coffee) for income generation as a means of supporting women’s livelihoods. Community consultations (workshops and meetings) will identify and prioritize opportunities, ensuring a community-driven approach to the development of value-added products.

2. Activity 2.4.3 will deliver outreach and educational information on recommended value-added production techniques and materials to individual target communities based on relevance. Assistance with production and distribution of value-added products (e.g., dried fruits, dried nuts, smoked fish, shell crabs, copra, coffee) for income generation as a means of supporting women’s livelihoods will be provided through the establishment of women-led producer groups established at the community and/or Area Level council. In collaboration with the Government Climate Change Secretariat (GCCS), private sector linkages will be facilitated to ensure that private sector funding becomes readily available when delivered by GGCF, private sector partnerships will be identified and established to further support community-level enterprises and enhance their capacity for business development. This will include providing technical assistance or small enterprises (including support to identify longer term sources of finance for business development).

Consideration of Gender Equality and Social Inclusion (GESI) in climate change adaptation actions is critical to ensuring that women, men, youth, and other communities are included in creating climate-resilient livelihoods in the rural areas. This component is key to ensuring the sustainability of the adaptation packages approach, by embedding the system of community-based adaptation into the existing provincial and national planning processes and budgeting cycles. The component will also build the capacity of provincial and national actors to access and use climate information for decision-making.

### GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 14

#### Activity 2.1.3 will utilise co-finance from Australia's Department of Foreign Affairs and Trade to provide technical assistance to the National Disaster Management Office for the design of a government oversight mechanism for disaster risk management in Vanuatu. This will include:

- **Specialized procured party** to develop and pilot locally appropriate targeting criteria based on needs (including inputs from Activity 1.2.2) and triggers determined by national-level indicators of shock such as drought, cyclones, floods, or earthquakes.
- **A database** will be used to register the most vulnerable children and households in communities. Targeting criteria chosen for this activity will be informed by lessons learned from other activities (e.g., Activity 1.2.3) and further strengthen sub-national CIDS and EWS systems (including via output 1.3). The system consists of pre-existing national-level systems that can be adapted to the local context, including a mechanism for acute needs and stress. Delivery will occur through physical payment, vouchers or mobile money, to be transferred directly into households' bank accounts.

#### Output 2.2: Enhanced local-national linkages through knowledge management and creation of feedback loops

- This output will support project-supported processes and outcomes are shared between communities, Area Councils, Provinces and the national level with a view to building a culture of adaptation learning in Vanuatu at provincial and national levels for successful adaptation actions.

#### Activities under this output will result in:

- The production and distribution of 30 annual knowledge management products, including local language materials.
- Engagement in 15 national, regional and/or international forums to disseminate local CBA information.
- Systemized capture, evaluation, and reporting of data on CDRRR sector policy implementation by at least 23 Area Councils.
- Annual meetings/forums held, increasing communication vertically across government to increase inclusion of local needs in planning decisions.

#### Activity descriptions

##### 3.2.1: Capture lessons learned, emerging themes and best practices at the community level to ensure sub-national and national planning processes are informed by lessons and that local actors identify national objectives

- **Activity 3.2.1 will promote national and sub-national planning processes that are informed by community experiences (such as capturing lessons learned, emerging themes and best practices) and that community-level actors are in support of national public policy objectives**.
- Periodic evaluations will be conducted to ensure the effectiveness of the CDRRR sector policy implementation at both the national and provincial levels. These evaluations will also document lessons learned on the successes and challenges of project implementation. Bespoke knowledge management products will be developed based on the evaluation findings, including the knowledge gained from evaluations (including impact assessments) directly to national stakeholders (including via output 1.3).
- Participation of community champions in national and regional forums on climate change adaptation governance and disaster risk reduction will be supported as a means of increasing communication and linkages between community-level and national stakeholders. Dissemination of knowledge management

## 3.2: Support Local authorities in monitoring and evaluation of national CCDR policies at the local level and support reporting on implementation of national sector policies

### 3.2.1: Establish a system to support local authorities in monitoring and evaluating national sector policies on climate change and disaster risk reduction, further working to strengthen linkages and communication between subnational, provincial and national stakeholders.

Subnational authorities (including the Provincial Council and provincial officials) will be supported through training and capacity building to support reporting on implementation of the national CCDR policy. This will be done within local stakeholders' respective subnational planning processes with strong engagement with civil society (including Vanuatu Climate Change Alliance, VCCA). Participatory events will be facilitated between subnational authorities and national stakeholders.
