## B.A. Implementation arrangements (max. 1500 words, approximately 2 pages plus diagrams)

The legal agreement between the GCF and Save the Children Australia (SCA) as Accredited Entity (AE) will be a grant agreement through to FAAR, SCA, as the AE, will enter into legally binding sub- agreements with other government entities such as the Ministry of Agriculture, Livestock, Forestry and Fisheries and Biosecurity (MABF), Ministry of Internal Affairs, Ministry of Agriculture, Livestock, Forestry, Fisheries and Biosecurity, Ministry of Land Management, Environment and Tourism (MLMET) and others. The sub-agreements with other ministers will be managed through both the established Government Investment Program (GIP) system as well as intergovernmental MoUs between MiCC and other government entities. These agreements will specify: (1) the purpose and objectives of the project; sources and destination of funds; (3) detailed budget; (4) specifics of project objectives and activities; (5) beneficiary groups; (6) monitoring, evaluation and reporting requirements. The GIPs will be integrated into the Government Financial Management Information System (MFIS) and linked to the Single Window for Reporting (SWR). Any funds that are not spent by the end of the project period or funded activities not delivered through Government Ministries or the EEs will be undertaken through private sector entities. These agreements will specify: (1) the purpose and objectives of the project; sources and destination of funds; (3) detailed budget; (4) specifics of project objectives and activities; (5) beneficiary groups; (6) monitoring, evaluation and reporting requirements. The GIPs will be annexes to the MoUs. No funds will be directly disbursed to community members in cash. All relevant goods and services will be


---
**Page 41**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 19

B

procured via co-EEIs or government partners. Note: any investment income earned on interest from the EES GCF Bank accounts will be retrofitted into the GCF.

### Project implementation will be facilitated by the key stakeholder departments contained within the Vanuatu Climate Change Secretariat (VCCS), Department of Environment and Natural Resources Change (DNGC), Department of Local Authorities (DLA), Department of Agriculture and Rural Development (DARD), Vanuatu Fisheries Authority (VFA), Ministry of Health and Medical Services (MHMS), Ministry of Women's Affairs (DWA), Vanuatu Meteorology and Geo-Hazards Department (VMGD), and the National Disaster Management Office (NDMO). The VCCS will be responsible for coordinating with these agencies to deliver agreed components of the adaptation package. Save the Children Vanuatu will also deliver several components of the project, including capacity building and awareness raising activities in partnership with private sector and civil society partners in all six provinces. The MoCC will retain overall responsibility for the implementation of the project.

### Agreements between the executing Entities and beneficiary communities will be captured in the Community Adaptation Plans before investments are made in the communities. The plans are developed by Save the Children Vanuatu (SCV) with input from the MoCC, VCCS, and other relevant staff. Once agreed they are approved by the Executing Entities. Once approved, they are implemented as outlined below.

### Save the Children Vanuatu (SCV) and the MoCC will work with other government ministries and procured parties to deliver agreed national-level activities. Where required civil society and private sector partners may be engaged in these activities. The SCV will also provide technical assistance where it has a comparative advantage in consultation with the Vanuatu Chamber of Commerce and the Vanuatu Business Association (VBA). These agreements are captured in the Community Adaptation Plans.

### Co-financing from the Government of Australia will be managed through a grant agreement to SCA. A duplicate copy of this agreement is held by SCV and the MoCC. Funds will flow from SCA to SCV in the same way as all GCF funds, via the General Account (GA) at the Bank of Vanuatu in Port Vila. Centralized oversight and reporting is managed and documented through the AMS.

### Co-financing from the Government of Vanuatu to the project is managed through the GIP system (GIP System), which is a financial management information system (FMIS), and linked to the Single Treasury Account (STA) for oversight and reporting against projects.

### The co-executing Entities will work together through a single Project Management Unit (PMU) and technical team based in Port Vila, within the Department of Environment Change (DNGC). The PMU will maintain clear lines of communication with the MoCC and the NDA/VMGD during project implementation; undertake project-wide M&E; provide technical support to implementing partners on climate change adaptation and disaster risk reduction; and oversee environmental and social safeguards (ESS) including monitoring the project's environmental performance and supporting the integration of gender considerations. The PMU will also be responsible for overall coordination, communication, and coordination with other projects, donors and organizations (see Figures 9 and 9.1 below).

132. The PMU will be led by a Team Leader reporting to the SCV Country Director and Director-General (DG) of Ministry of Climate Change. The Team Leader will line manage the other PMU members including a Finance and Compliance Specialist, Procurement Specialist, MEAL Specialist, ESS/GESI Specialist, Communications Specialist, Component Coordinators and Technical Advisers.


---
**Page 43**
---

133\. A Project Steering Committee (PSC) will be formed and will be responsible for overall strategic oversight of the project. The PSC will support strategic high-level coordination and oversight and promote a culture of learning within the project among senior country counterparts. The PSC will also provide a high-level forum for discussion, review and providing feedback on performance reports produced by the PMU\. It will make decisions on critical project issues raised by Technical Advisers and other stakeholders. The PSC will be responsible for reviewing and approving management improvement actions arising from audits and addressing serious project quality and implementation issues. The PSC will also provide guidance to the PMU on how to address a specific project, such as sensitive safeguarding issues that cannot be addressed by TWG\. Project performance reports will be submitted quarterly to the PSC with an emphasis on regular reporting, strong coordination and transparency.

134\. The PSC will be chaired by the DG for Ministry of Climate Change and Country Director for SCV and will be comprised of all agency Directors involved in project implementation (DGCC, DLAD, DARD, VFD, VMGG, NMDO, DoWRR, DWAR). Secretaries General of provincial governments, VCPO PMU Team Leader, Project Manager, and the Chairperson of the National Climate Change Resilience Council. It is proposed that the PSC will meet twice each year (with extraordinary meetings collected as needed).

135\. The NDA for Vanuatu is the Director General for the Ministry of Climate Change (DG MoCC). As the Coordinating Lead Agency, the DG MoCC will be responsible for overall coordination and oversight. The DG MoCC as NDA is governed by the National Advisory Board (NAB) which is the decision-making mechanism for the NDA. The NAB is chaired by the DG MoCC with a multi-stakeholder body of representatives from across Ministries\. The role of the DG MoCC as EE for the project is governed by the PSC with oversight for the Minister of MoCC\. Conflict of interest is avoided by both mechanisms being separate.

136\. The PSC shall report and provide updates to the NAB when it meets 3-4 times per year. The NAB is responsible for providing strategic direction, guidance and oversight on the project implementation together to promote greater levels of coordination among the many individual climate change and disaster risk reduction projects being implemented in Vanuatu\. The NAB will also be responsible for supporting the functionality of the NAB through technical support to the NAB Secretariat and ensure the PSC is accountable to the NAB throughout the project duration.

137\. A National Technical Working Group (NTWG) will be formed to provide operational and technical oversight during implementation of the project. The NTWG will meet quarterly to provide feedback to the PMU on project progress, quality assurance, and other issues impacting the project. It will also provide input to the PMU and to address critical issues, discuss and provide feedback on recommendations from previous meetings. The NTWG will be comprised of representatives from across government agencies with issues impacting delivery (including significant complaints and feedback from communities) and project stakeholders. The NTWG will include representatives from all levels of government including key government and non-government agencies (not senior management / Director level positions).

138\. Provincial Technical Working Groups (PTWG) will also be formed in each province\. This body will be responsible for providing oversight on provincial project quality and implementation issues to the NTWG. This group will also meet on a quarterly basis and will provide feedback to the NTWG, PMU and other stakeholders.

139\. The Provincial Technical Advisory Group (PTAG) will be comprised of representatives from provincial authorities such as Area Administrators and Community Liaison Officers as required.

140\. The proposed implementation structure for project operations is provided in Figure 10 below. Note the EES will maintain final decision-making in respect of the implementation of the Project activities.

Accredited Entity Capacity: experience and track record
1. Save the Children is the world's leading independent organization for children, with 30 national organizations in more than 120 countries. In 2019, Save the Children delivered programs worth over USD 2.2 billion across 117 countries and directly reached over one million children.
2. Save the Children Australia (SCA) is accredited to the GCF on November 2019 on behalf of the SCA Group. The accreditation was granted in recognition of our long-standing commitment, as well as our longstanding leadership role in climate change and Disaster Risk Reduction.
3. SCA has extensive experience of designing, delivering, evaluating and documenting approaches to community-based adaptation, including in Vanuatu, where we have worked for more than 25 years.

Executing Fostering Capacity: experience and track record
The project is closely aligned to, and supported by, key national and senior policy makers and strategies in Vanuatu. The project will be implemented through two implementing Entities: SCA and Save the Children Vanuatu (SCV). Both entities have extensive experience in managing and delivering climate change programs across Vanuatu at a range of scales and from a variety of funding sources (including the Global Environment Facility, the United Nations Development Programme, the World Bank, and the GCF). SCV is managed by SCA and utilizes the AE's systems and processes.


---
**Page 45**
---

The Vanuatu MACC is the co-executing entity for GCF FP035 via the Department of Meteorology and Geohazard(s) and is the executing entity for a number of donor-funded projects, including via the GEF.

A review conducted by experts from the Australian Department of Foreign Affairs and Trade for the Department of Meteorology and Geohazard(s), in collaboration with the Vanuatu Ministry of Finance, found that MoG has the experience and expertise to carry out the scope of work outlined in the project successfully. The results of the assessment highlighted that Save the Children should work together with MoG to develop a comprehensive set of procurement guidelines, including planning and budgeting, reporting, controls, and staffing. Similarly, while the team found that MoG has the capacity to manage the project, it recommended that MoG review its procurement guidelines, which were deemed to be adequate as outlined in their procurement manuals, but the team identified some areas where MoG could improve on the basis of the Vanuatu context.

The assessment also highlighted that MoG should ensure that all procurement activities are carried out within the approved budget. While the review identified no red flag risks, Save the Children will continue to collaborate with MoG and the Department of Meteorology and Geohazard(s) in developing a comprehensive set of procurement guidelines for the project. The team recommended that MoG carry out an additional compliance review of the consistency of the application of the guidelines prior to the commencement of the project.


* Government of Vanuatu (2016) Climate public expenditure and institutional review report, Vanuatu, Australia 2016

B.5. Justification of GCF funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

### B.5.1. To date, the majority of climate change adaptation projects in Vanuatu have either worked at the national level on capacity building and policy development or piloted community-based approaches in isolated locations. The Government of Vanuatu has not yet developed a comprehensive approach to community-based adaptation (CBA) across the country as the GEF-supported Vanuatu Coastal Adaptation Project (VCAP) is currently the only major project that includes CBA activities.

This trend holds true for the broader Pacific region and across the developing world, where the vast majority of CBA initiatives have been small scale, short term and limited in reach. Notable examples of ongoing or completed projects are provided in Annex 1 (small scale) and Annex 2 (medium to large scale phase and closing/launching phase) are provided in Annex 2.

### B.5.2. As highlighted by the VCAP project, CBA is a key component of Vanuatu’s national and sub-national response to climate change. As also noted, accessing finance to support CBA initiatives has been challenging for communities in Vanuatu due to limited access to financial services and civil society. Communities in Vanuatu are some of the least responsible for the emissions that are causing climate change yet they will be among the most vulnerable to its impacts, particularly those more acutely than people in wealthier, less hazard-exposed countries[1]. The Government struggles to balance the broad range of adaptation priorities (from protecting critical economic infrastructure to building resilience and capacity at the community level) with limited resources. There is a clear argument for high co-finance and private sector engagement to support CBA initiatives that are not only cost-effective but also have the potential to generate actions in response to a problem stemming from the unregulated use of the global commons, or to take action on behalf of those who cannot afford to do so.

The beneficiaries are communities on low-lying islands facing an ever-higher natural tide of their own making. These communities are not only among the most vulnerable but also among the poorest in sectors, but governments struggle to find the resources to meet current development needs within constrained budgets and limited access to financial services. The total amount requested from the GCF for this project is commensurate with the scale of the problem and the Vanuatu Government’s desire to take a coordinated nation-wide approach to addressing community-based adaptation.

### B.5.3. Despite global climate finance continuing to increase over time the amount allocated to adaptation has remained relatively small (approximately 10% of total global climate finance, but less than 5% of total climate finance)[2]. While it is difficult to accurately account for the total amount of climate finance dedicated to CBA initiatives, the World Bank estimates that only about 3.4% of climate finance from dedicated multilateral, bilateral and regional climate funds between 2008-2017 was allocated to CBA activities[3]. The Global Environment Facility (GEF) Secretariat estimated this figure was around USD 1.5 billion, of which around USD 1.3 billion was allocated to CBA activities – equating to an average of approximately USD 64 million allocated to CBA annually since 2007. This represents a very small proportion (less than 0.2%) of total global climate finance flows for adaptation – still an insignificant figure when taken as a percentage of public climate finance flows for adaptation.

Within these total flows, some funds and donors have attempted to prioritize CBA, but only at small scale. For example, the GEF has developed a specific funding mechanism – the Small Grants Programme (SGP), which channels funding direct to communities and civil society organizations in developing countries. The SGP is an innovative approach that provides regulated mechanism; however, individual grants are capped at USD 50,000, necessarily resulting in very small-scale pilot activities. In Vanuatu, for example, the SGP has supported 60 projects since 2007[4]. While these small scale CBA initiatives can have a significant impact on local lives, but they are not likely to result in transformational change.

[1] The Government of Vanuatu has had, including in NDCs that significant external finance will be required if the country is to effectively manage the unavoidable impacts of climate change.

[3] The World Bank (2017). Global Environment Facility (GEF) 2015: Climate Finance Initiative. GCF Secretariat Working Paper. Available [here](https://www.greenclimate.fund/sites/default/files/2016-03/GCF_Secretariat_WP_2014.pdf).

13. With the level of need at the local level growing and the majority of donors choosing to remain focused on national level programmes, there is a significant gap in climate finance that the GCF is well positioned to fill. This gap is particularly acute for CBA activities which are often small-scale, pilot approach that struggles to make an impact beyond individual communities. With GCF support, it will be possible to scale up and replicate these approaches at national level, driving transformative change in this way adaptation is conceived from the bottom-up, and provide the donor community with a more effective vehicle for supporting climate resilience building community resilience. The Government of Vanuatu is committed to CBA approaches.

14. With the current GCF funding request, 20% of the total project cost will be allocated towards building community resilience. The Government of Vanuatu is committed to CBA approaches and these are expected to be scaled up across a wide range of sectors which are unlikely to focal sufficient resources to catalyse such CBA activities.

15. There is a strong justification for GCF funding request. The additional burdens on communities' livelihoods, particularly in rural areas, will continue to increase as climate change impacts become more pronounced and grow over time. Sudden onset events, like TC Pam and Cyclone Harold, have resulted in economic dislocation and loss of assets, with the most vulnerable communities being those living in remote areas (over 64% of Vanuatu's GDP), with rural and remote communities particularly affected. The need for these communities to adapt is urgent.

16. The current GCF funding request will support the Government of Vanuatu to scale up CBA activities which are only slowly beginning down and is currently not on track to achieve the Paris Agreement's goal of keeping global temperature well below 2°C. The project work will be implemented in 30 communities (of the rural population) which is equivalent to 15% of the total rural population. A further 119,000 rural people will be indirectly reached - increasing the projects total reach to 95% of the rural population. Meeting this additional burden on communities' livelihoods and adapting to climate change in a sustainable manner is not available via other mechanisms.

17. It is clear that the Government of Vanuatu does not currently have sufficient funding from any sources (including domestic resources, donor funds and global climate finance) to meet immediate adaptation needs and build capacity for future climate resilience building activities across its entire rural population. It is equally clear that donor funds are not an adequate source of CBA finance as they are shorter term and smaller scale. Grant funding is the most appropriate level of concessionality for CBA activities, but it does not provide the long-term financial sustainability or build capacity. CBA activities that are not funded directly by government or cannot be self-funded by communities are unlikely to be sustainable in the longer term. The Government of Vanuatu's current budget does not have the capacity to meet adaptation needs and rural communities do not have the resources to adapt.

B.5 Exit strategy (max 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The VCDCC package under Component 2 is designed to be modular and scalable. This makes the package approach highly tailored to direct beneficiary needs and transferable to the remaining communities in Vanuatu. The CBA activities are designed to be self-sustaining and are designed to ensure the sustainability of the adaptation project actions and help government to support a scale up approach over time.

Component 2: DLA activities. A study identified opportunities during implementation to have CDCCCs captured in the Decentralization Act formality recognized in the service delivery structure. This will provide a strong foundation for the future scaling up of CBA activities as key partners for the project will support by strengthening and demonstrating the value of the CDCCCs.

B

**projects have proved highly successful. However, many have been small scale and have struggled to gain traction beyond targeted communities. This project aims to build a substantial evidence base of climate change adaptation practices that can be scaled across the region. It will reduce the likelihood of future climate finance flows in Vanuatu including an allocation for support CBA activities. This will be achieved via activities in Component 3 (i.e., 3.2.1), the project’s MEAL (monitoring, evaluation and learning) plan, and through a series of workshops to disseminate knowledge products to a range of stakeholders. The project will also work to support the Department of Local Authorities (DoLA) in Vanuatu in developing a climate change adaptation strategy that can be taken on as permanent staff in the DoLA at the provincial or Area Council level.**

### **Alleviating Vulnerability and Promoting Local Ownership**

The project will work with provincial governments to integrate the risks of climate change as well as current and future adaptation actions into local planning and budgetary processes such as those experienced in selected communities. The project will also support the Department of Local Authorities (DoLA) in Vanuatu, learned with NAB and other national stakeholders to foster scaling-up of the actions on a national scale. This will be achieved by building capacity at provincial level to better understand the needs and capacities of communities and increase the likelihood of domestic resources being allocated for climate change adaptation. The project will also support the DoLA in Vanuatu so that communities are better positioned to access domestic resources for adaptation action. As Vanuatu is currently experiencing a high level of financial constraints due to the COVID-19 pandemic, adaptation priorities at the community level may find domestic funding sources such as the expenditure on climate change adaptation projects and activities. The project will ensure that climate finance will remain a key source of adaptation support for the foreseeable future. A further key aim of this project is to build capacity in provincial governments so they are better positioned to cross future flows of climate finance for adaptation action regardless of the source.

The project will deliver training to 145 government staff on integrating climate change risks and adaptation actions into local planning and budgetary processes, as well as supporting them to undertake adaptive budgeting processes during project implementation to influence future processes. This will be achieved by building capacity at provincial level to better understand the needs and capacities of communities and increase the likelihood of domestic resources being allocated for climate change adaptation action.

### **Promoting Local Ownership**

All activities implemented at the community level will be based on community stakeholder engagement combined with climate impact assessments. Communities will determine their own priorities, which are informed by the project team from the inception package. This ownership over project activity implementation at the community level will increase the likelihood of successful adaptation action and ensure that communities are better positioned to cross future flows of climate finance for adaptation action regardless of the source.

Stronger and more resilient food systems will reduce the impact of tropical cyclones and reduce the vulnerability of communities in Vanuatu. The project will support the Department of Local Authorities (DoLA) in Vanuatu and beyond that has shown that the more engaged and in control community stakeholders are in how they manage their resources, the better positioned they are to adapt to climate change. This approach is also in line with the Principles for Locally-led Adaptation™ developed by a partnership between the World Bank and the Government of Vanuatu. The Government of Vanuatu has endorsed the principles and will endeavor to fulfill them in our climate finance projects.

### **Ensure Financial Sustainability of the Project**

The project will aim to maximise GCF financing to address adaptation needs, while also ensuring that communities are better positioned to cross future introduced adaptation technologies (including solar PV freezers and solar air driers) to reduce the likelihood of future climate finance flows in Vanuatu. Detailed strategies are included in the project activities that include training for target communities on installation, maintenance and repair of the technologies as well as providing technical support or parts or training using local materials for repairs and maintenance. This guidance on post-sale-repair and maintenance will be provided to ensure that communities have the capacity to procure adaptation technology services for use in communities. Targeted trainings will also be provided to address long-term maintenance and operation challenges. A further key component

B

The capacity of government and civil society systems to enable improved service delivery of climate change adaptation initiatives. The project will strengthen the strengthening the national and local government and civil society systems and staff, strengthening the nationally endorsed development plans and strategies for climate change adaptation and disaster risk reduction. For example, fiduciary arrangements for the project will utilize a de-centralized financial management approach with the Ministry of Finance and Treasury and the Ministry of Agriculture and Livestock. Similarly, the project will utilize Area Council structures and processes for implementation at the provincial level. The project will also support the development of a new national climate change adaptation strategy (which may vary in effectiveness currently amongst Ministries and Area Councils) will provide improved coordination mechanisms within the 29 targeted Area Councils and increase the effectiveness of local government service delivery through the establishment of a new National Climate Change Adaptation Strategy. The satellite internet connectivity supported by the project is key to this increased coordination, cooperation, as real time data and information can flow in both directions. The connectivity will be sustained over the long term.

Faster sustainable value chains in the local market: Food surpluses from agriculture and fisheries are expected to be generated due to improvements in agricultural and fisheries practices, including through improved access to markets and enhanced access to inputs such as fertilizers and seeds, and improved handling of produce. Food production is essential due to the long distances and limited infrastructure for transporting food products within the country and between the outer islands of Vanuatu. Establishing the value chains ensures a small but steady supply of income to the common people in the outer islands of Vanuatu. The project will support the development of these value chains, which are expected to be sustained over the long term. Once communities have realized the potential of marketing food surplus products through the project, they can continue to benefit from this increased market support, especially through fostering of tenuring arrangements between local producers and private sector entities. For example, in the food supply chain, engagement in the project and the creation of new commercial supply chains is expected as it will be more sustainable following the COVID-19 pandemic. Further, in collaboration with the VCO, private sector partners, and other stakeholders, the project will support the development of value chains and enhance access to markets, and where possible, support producer groups to transition into micro or small-scale enterprises (supporting commercially viable opportunities for financial resource development).

The project's implementation will be supported by government and Save the Children. The project will implement this through: 1) increasing the ministry and provincial governments' capacity to support adaptation at the community level; 2) substantially strengthening local implementation structures, including Area Councils, to ensure that they are able to effectively plan for climate change actions and opportunities. Support local producer groups to access markets for processed and private sector entities as an incentive to be involved in projects funded by the project through engaging VCO support.

### D. EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

This section reviews the performance of proposed projects against the investment criteria as set out in the GCF's Initial Investment Framework.

#### 1. Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The project will contribute to the GCF’s overarching adaptation impact – contributing to increased climate-resilient sustainable development – by directly increasing the climate resilience of smallholder farmers and fishers in six provinces of Vanuatu, who are among the most vulnerable and socially marginalized group. Direct beneficiaries at community level will benefit from improved access to markets, financial instruments, agricultural and climate technical advice and who suffer from poor infrastructure and limited access to services due to their remote location and disability. Beneficiary numbers were calculated via a systematic approach to beneficiary identification as Area 1 (Agriculture) and Area 2 (Fisheries) are defined in Annexes 1 and 3.

The project will also contribute to the GCF’s overarching adaptation impact – contributing to increased climate-resilient sustainable development – by directly increasing the climate resilience of smallholder farmers and fishers at provincial level. Beneficiaries at provincial level will benefit from improved access to markets, financial instruments, agricultural and climate technical advice and who suffer from poor infrastructure and limited access to services due to their remote location and disability.

The project will also contribute to several of the GCF’s Fund-level adaptation and enabling environment deliverables:

- **Area 1A**: Most vulnerable people and communities as it targets some of the most climate vulnerable remote areas in Vanuatu.
- **Area 2A**: Health, well-being, food and water security as it will help significantly strengthen local-level climate resilience via increased food security, diversified livelihoods strategies and reduced disease burden.
- **Area 4A**: Ecosystems and ecosystem services as it will help protect and rehabilitate critical ecosystem services in the most vulnerable areas of Vanuatu.
- **Core indicator 5 (Degree)**: Where GCF investments contribute to strengthening institutional and regulatory frameworks for low emission climate-resilient development pathways in a country-driven manner, including through capacity building, policy dialogue, knowledge sharing, planning and implementation coordination mechanisms.

The project will also increase the GCF’s reach at the local level – highlighting the Fund’s ability to directly impact the lives of vulnerable people with practical adaptation actions that measurably build climate resilience. The project will work nation-wide, reaching 44% of the rural population (which is in itself a significant achievement) and increasing the project’s total reach to 95% of the rural population. The entire population of Vanuatu lives in remote areas with poor infrastructure and limited access to services due to their remote location and disability.

The project will also contribute to several of the GCF’s Fund-level adaptation and enabling environment deliverables:

- **Core indicator 2 (Degree)**: Where GCF investments contribute to climate deployment, dissemination and scaling-up through support by supporting introduction of climate resilient food production and processing tools, systems and technologies; and
- **Core indicator 3 (Degree)**: Where GCF investments contribute to knowledge generation and learning processes, and use of good practices, methodologies and standards)

The project will result in substantial reductions in the risk of loss of lives and assets due to reduced exposure to extreme weather events, as well as a significant increase in the number of people adopting climate-resilient livelihoods options. ### D2\. Paradigm shift potential (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)

The project will lead to a paradigm shift away from an agricultural system that is highly sensitive to climate change impacts and that does not ensure food and water security for farmers and fishers. Achieving reduced vulnerability and increased resilience requires a shift in agricultural systems towards more sustainable, resilient, and productive ones that promote communities resilient to the impacts of climate change, through a transformation in the way adaptation actions are designed, implemented, monitored, and evaluated. This will be achieved by providing information and perceptions; empowering them to make informed decisions about the nature, timing and type of adaptation measures they need to take; and ensuring that these decisions are taken at all levels.

This results upward through governance structures to create a fundamental shift in the way adaptation is pursued. It is important to note that this transformation will not be achieved by communities acting at level that are not community “owned” and, therefore, not consistently maintained or sustained after project funding concludes.

The project will also contribute to many of the transformational successes identified by the Government of Vanuatu in their GCF country programme**, including: a demand-driven bottom-up approach that meets local needs; an integrated approach that addresses multiple climate change impacts; purpose solutions (without maladaptation); transparent selection of project beneficiaries; transparent selection of project partners; and the inclusion of smallholder farmers, subsistence farmers, small and medium enterprises (SMEs), communication and outreach, meaningful engagement with CSOs and NGOs, and leveraging CCF and other climate finance into annual development programmes, planning and policy.

The project aims to result in a step change in the scale at which GCF is conceived and implemented. Vanuatu has already demonstrated that it can successfully implement projects on a large scale with sufficient implementation time to ensure community structures are built and have the capacity to continue beyond the life of the project. The project will also contribute to the Fund’s strategic objective also aims for a transformation in the accessibility of climate finance at the community level. Too often climate finance is not accessible or available to communities, particularly those that are remote; communities are ready to access and effectively utilize future flows of climate finance from a range of sources.

The project will help overcome information, technical, financial, social and institutional barriers that prevent communities from accessing climate finance. It will do this by implementing measures that will: improve the resilience of local production systems; increase the efficiency of agricultural practices through improved technologies and services; and strengthen national coordination and local governance capacity. The project is also transformative in its focus on promoting gender equality, youth empowerment and participation in decision making by women, youth and people with disability.

The present model for scaling up and replication will be tested in Vanuatu. It will provide lessons to scale up CBA approaches in one country will provide a platform for replication across different contexts. This model will seek to

## Institutional knowledge and learning - Part of a larger component will be dedicated to knowledge sharing and learning engagement between national and sub-national government within Vanuatu via a new National Climate Change Secretariat (NCCS) and the development of a climate change unit in the government within existing governance structures in Vanuatu and ultimately the knowledge generated through this project will be shared with other Pacific Island countries.

### Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment - In order to provide for the longer-term sustainability of any adaptation technology provided to communities, the project will ensure adaptive capacity is built into the systems and processes. Importantly, the project will ensure that government systems and processes are adapted to support climate change adaptation in a way that is consistent with the current vision of decentralization. This is a key paradigm shift in a country that has largely been governed by centralised structures for many years.

Under Output 2.4 we will target communities diversify their livelihood options via supporting access to markets and services, as well as providing training and capacity building opportunities for communities to engage with private sector entities and increase the sustainability of project activities.

### Contribution to the regulatory framework and policies - Enhanced dialogue processes will help reality check national policies and ensure they are targeted at effectively meeting the needs of communities. The project will also support the development of a climate change adaptation policy that integrates climate change risks and resilience building actions into development planning and budgeting, with a view towards supporting a sustainable transition to locally resource managed responsive planning and development.

### Overall contribution to climate-resilient development pathways consistent with a country’s climate vulnerability profile - The project will be designed in such a way as to have both scalability and replicability, aligned with, and designed to support achievement of the objectives of the national adaptation plan. It is envisaged that the project will be scaled up to other communities via local authorities. Provincial government officials, trained in climate focused stakeholder engagement processes, will be able to replicate this approach within their jurisdiction beyond the project to non-targeted communities. Within the region, the initial outcomes of this project will inform the development of the four partners’ adaptation and related issues at the community level.

### D.3 Summary of Outputs (continued) - 500 pages

17. Undertaking adaptation programming at the community level requires a holistic view of community needs and capacities. At the local level, the impacts of climate change are increasingly interrelated with broader development challenges. It is clear that there is no single solution to address existing development challenges and that, in the absence of significant adaptation action, sustainable development will be undermined. The project will therefore focus on building communities’ capacity by reducing vulnerability and increasing resilience to the current and projected impacts of climate change. This will enable communities to adapt to a changing environment so that they have a stable base from which to increase resilience to projected impacts and build adaptive capacity for future challenges.

17. The adaptation package includes actions with a range of economic, social, environmental and gender co-benefits, including:

- Increasing the livelihood incomes of rural communities through livelihoods diversification and increasing access to markets (co-benefit 1);
- Increasing food security in rural communities by ensuring communities have more secure access to locally available nutritious foods (co-benefit 2);
- Increasing access to education by ensuring fewer school days lost in the aftermath of extreme weather events through adaptation planning and enhanced DRR action (co-benefit 3).

### D.4 Needs of recipient
(max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)
Vanuatu leads the world disaster risk index as the country with the highest disaster risk score of 96.4 (out of a possible 100). Vanuatu is highly vulnerable to climate change impacts due to its low-lying coastal areas and high vulnerability to cyclones, storm surges, sea level rise, and increased frequency of extreme weather events. The country faces significant adaptation deficits and build community resilience to the current and anticipated impacts of climate change poses a significant challenge in Vanuatu. As such, the government has prioritized integrating climate change risks and resilience building into core objectives.

### Absence of alternative livelihoods
The project will help to address this through strengthening institutional capacity.
Vanuatu recently graduated from the LDC category to a lower middle-income country; it remains highly aid-dependent. Domestic revenue remains insufficient to adequately fund essential services, including disaster risk management. The project will contribute towards addressing immediate development priorities (like economic growth, poverty reduction, health and education), with a focus on climate change adaptation.

### Climate Change
The project aims to increase the resilience of communities in Vanuatu by integrating climate change risks and resilience building into core objectives. The capacity of line ministries and other relevant agencies to effectively manage climate change is limited; however, Government has access to strong technical climate change capacity, the capacity to deliver

181 The economic impacts of COVID-19 have been dire in Vanuatu, while the Country has recorded only a handful of cases and no deaths. However, the pandemic has led to reduced tourism revenue and reduced tax receipts pushed the domestic budget deficit in deficit in 2020/21 and is likely to result in increased poverty and food insecurity for many households.

The initial problem identification was derived from the deep experience of Save the Children and the MoCC in working at all levels across the country to understand capacities, needs and gaps particularly with respect to climate change adaptation. This informed the development of projects. During the detailed design phase, extensive consultations were undertaken at the national, provincial and community level. These consultations built on the lessons learned from previous projects and existing understanding of the issues facing communities. These consultations revealed significant climate change related challenges that are not being adequately addressed by current interventions. There were also key issues associated with limited or declining community infrastructure and cultural practices that need to be considered in any adaptation response, particularly within and between islands. This was supported by national and sub-national consultations, that confirmed the need for a flexible approach to address these critical adaptation challenges.

The project is specifically designed to achieve national climate-relevant objectives of key development partners, including the MoCC, NAPA, CCRDR, and NAPCC, through close cooperation with senior staff from key departments as well as the Vanuatu MoCC – the project’s co-executor. Adaptation is the key focus of Vanuatu’s approach to addressing climate change. It is its first National Development Plan (NDP) priority and is also a key component of the NAPA, CCRDR and NAPCC.

In response to the climate threats facing Vanuatu, the National Adaptation Programme for Action (NAPA), Climate Change Coordination and Risk Reduction Policy (CCRDR) and National Adaptation Plan (NAP) are being developed. The NAPA identifies five adaptation priorities areas for Vanuatu: namely; i) agriculture and food security; ii) sustainable tourism development; iii) community-based management of natural resources; iv) disaster risk reduction and climate change adaptation; v) coastal zone management. This project will help address NAPA priorities (i), (ii) and (iv).

The National Climate Change Adaptation Strategy (NCCAS) 2015-2030, the National Climate Change Action Plan (NAPCC) (2012-2017) was designed to build on the NAPA, the Priorities and Action Agenda (PPA) and the Disaster Risk Reduction Policy. The National Climate Change Adaptation Strategy 2015–2030 (NCCAS) builds on the work of the NAPCA, NCCAS, and other national climate change adaptation strategies. It is a strategic document that sets out the vision for climate change adaptation in Vanuatu, complementing the NAPA and CCRDR Policy and assisting the Government with prioritization of initiatives to address climate change impacts as part of its National Climate Change Adaptation Strategy Programme, including in the 2021 update.

Vanuatu’s NAPCA, CCRDR Policy and Country Priorities are fully aligned with the country’s National Strategic Development Plan (NSDP). The NSDP, the highest public policy framework in Vanuatu, explicitly includes climate change adaptation as a key sustainable development priority. The policy also recognizes that climate change is likely to have significant impacts on communities’ traditional agricultural practices, focusing on disaster risk reduction and climate change adaptation. More specifically, the NSDP sets out 10 national strategic objectives all related to adaptation, namely: ENV 3.1 Institutionalize climate change and disaster risk

The unregulated use of the global commons, or to take out concessional loans to do so, is less clear. It is even less strong when the ultimate beneficiaries are communities on low-lying islands facing an ever-hardening reality of climate change impacts. Investments in GBA activities are often difficult and sometimes impossible to find the resources to meet current development needs within communities, let alone provision for those anticipated future climate change impacts. The funding amount requested from the GCF for this project is $10 million. This is a significant amount of money that the Government of Vanuatu desires to take a nation-wide approach to addressing community adaptation needs.

### 134. In the context of Pacific Small Island Developing States (SIDS), most donor funding to date has focused on immediate adaptation needs at the local level. It is equally clear that most donor funds are not an adequate response to the scale and complexity of the challenges faced by communities in SIDS, which require the most appropriate level of concessionality for CBA activities as they are not of interest to the private sector financing climate change mitigation or direct resilience capacity. CBA activities are often funded directly by governments or are not financially viable without donor assistance.

In the context of Vanuatu, the government does not have the capacity to meet adaptation needs and rural communities in particular face significant challenges in accessing resources for adaptation. The community-based adaptation packages approach is embedded in government systems and scaled out to the remaining circuits of the country. However, there are still many communities that remain outside this system. Communities remaining to be targeted are less difficult to reach and, relatively, less vulnerable to the identified climate change impacts. This project will focus on these communities. The scale-up of this work – reaching some of the most vulnerable and hardest to access populations – allows the government to scale out the approach further to help meet the needs of slightly easier-to-reach communities.

### 134 Implementing projects in Pacific SIDS entails higher costs than in many other developing countries, as the islands are often small and isolated, located at the edge of the world’s largest ocean basins, large tracts of the Pacific Ocean, and environmental fragility results in very high cost of production of goods and services. The climate change impacts on agriculture, fishing, and tourism sectors that are heavily reliant to be the main source of formal sector employment and driver of economic activity in most Pacific SIDS, including Vanuatu, will have a significant impact on these communities which already face some of the most climate vulnerable communities in Vanuatu. These communities are often in the most remote and geographically isolated areas where access is difficult to reach them with resources needed to bring people and goods to them. These challenges are a key element of why these communities are highly vulnerable, and why they need support from the Government of Vanuatu. Providing assistance to such communities is, necessarily, high cost but, for a community-based adaptation project with the aim of meeting the immediate adaptation needs of the most vulnerable people, the cost is justifiable.

### 135 While there have been significant improvements in financial management systems and practices over recent years, the country has the Pacific region’s only functional Government Financial Management Information System (GFMIS). The GFMI system is a single entity with a single set of accounts and a single chart of accounts. This means that all Government ministries currently access all of their funds via this single account. This has resulted in a lack of transparency, accountability, and financial control and transparency and control that has enabled Vanuatu to be the first and, in many cases, only recipient of climate change adaptation funding from the Global Environment Facility (GEF), World Bank, DFAT, USAID, NZDMAT, UNICEF and others. The Vanuatu Government runs a fully centralized financial management system that has sufficient decentralized coverage through Government agencies in the islands to ensure that all funds are accounted for and controlled at the local level with the exception of funding. Project resources allocated to Government Exs (including Department of Local Authorities and Provincial Governments) are managed by the Ministry of Finance, which is responsible for Climate Change, and National Disaster Management Office and all flow through this centralized system.

### 136 The project will be designed to avoid over-burdening any one government delivery partner to avoid potential conflicts with other projects. This is a key lesson from the implementation of a range of previous projects implemented in Vanuatu and

197. An economic and financial analysis (Annex 3) undertaken as part of the project's design phase examined the economic impact of each of the four options for representation of the actions in the adaptation package menu:

* **EWI communications equipment (ERIR of 11%)**
* **Nature-based solutions – coastal management (ERIR of 12%)**
* **Climate resilient agriculture - land and yam as examples (ERIR of 19% for taro and 27% for yam)**
* **Food security and nutrition (ERIR of 30%)**

The analysis shows that all of the alternative measures have solid economic internal rates of return and can be justified on economic grounds. The analysis shows that the selected measures will have a significantly positive economic impact for the targeted communities over the life of the project and beyond.

The report also undertook assessment of the incremental adaptation benefits of the four selected measures against the baseline scenario, which is based on the current level of government spending at an economic internal rate of return of 3%, which, while positive, is below the discount rate. This is directly due to the high cost of delivering projects in Pacific SIDS and the need for significant additional investments, to ensure their long-term sustainability and to catalyse a paradigm shift in resilience in Vanuatu. However, the analysis found that the project still presents a strong investment for the GCF. An economic internal rate of return (ERIR) of 19% is achieved by the climate resilient agriculture option; and, therefore, while the full project shows a just slightly positive economic return on investment, each specific adaptation measure has an ERIR above 30%. This means that the project will ensure the project leaves a substantial legacy for capacity for sustainability and more effective allocation of resources in the future. The analysis also found that the incremental adaptation action on high risk constrained land-and-capacity contexts.

The analysis highlights three key reasons why the project Budget includes significant non-commodities investments, which are elaborated below as they are of fundamental importance to the project's success and ability to create transformational change in resilient communities:

1. The need to address significant capacity gaps and constraints at all levels (government at national level, communities at local level) for effective planning, implementation, monitoring and evaluation development and implementation of adaptation actions – particularly in highly climate vulnerable rural and remote communities.
2. The need to ensure that the project’s investments are sustainable over time. This first component is focused on building institutional capacity at the local level to ensure that the project's adaptation investments (delivered via the adaptation package menu under Component 2) are being implemented effectively, efficiently and sustainably in a way that ensures their long-term sustainability.
3. The need for significant non-commodities investments to provide additional catalytic functionality to increase the sustainability and replicability of the project’s adaptation investments and catalyse transformational change at scale. This is particularly important where communities are vital, and investments under Component 3 of the project focus on this element, as well as having a high cost of delivering projects in Pacific SIDS.

The high cost of delivering projects in Pacific SIDS is important to note that implementing projects in Pacific SIDS entails higher costs than in many other developing countries, as the 'combination

Component 3 of the project will be the key driver for institutional replicability of the projects approach by embedding the CSA planning and implementation and prioritization of actions into national and provincial planning and budgeting

## Sustainability

The Vanuatu Government committed to facilitating adaptation action at all levels. Adaptation in the long term will be a key element of the vulnerability assessment and will support the implementation of the National Vulnerability Operationalisation Assessment Framework (VAF).

| Medium |
| --- |

- The target scenario for the VCOB is that the Vanuatu Government will achieve NDC targets on livelihoods and food security by 2030.
- The vulnerability assessment will also support the development of a national adaptation plan to operationalize the National Vulnerability Operationalisation Assessment Framework (VAF).

### Key sustainability strategies

- Ensure project impact will be sustained over time.

Building the evidence base to inform future investment allocation to future climate finance and other funding streams, including driven adaptation actions.

### The country’s updated NDC includes revised targets and indicators aimed at enhancing the resilience of the agriculture and water sectors, developed in line with the objectives of mainstreaming food security, income security, and water security.

There is, however, no effective adaptation planning and implementation coordination mechanism in place to ensure community and national processes are inclusive. ### The adaptation packages, under Component 2, are designed to be modular and scalable. Specific activities can be initiated, developed, or scaled up, depending on community circumstances. This makes the package more flexible in terms of beneficiary needs and transferable to the private sector (indirect beneficiaries). Activities under Output 1.3 will ensure the sustainability of the adaptation package by supporting the government to adopt a scale-out of the model. The design of each package will be locally owned and based on local needs. As such, there is a high likelihood of achieving outcomes being sustainable over time.

### Activities under Output 2.4 (Women-led agricultural production and land preservation established to support food security) and Output 3.1 (In-schools options) will help support rural communities to access markets and reduce vulnerability to climate change, which will further increase the adaptive capacity of project-supported households.

### E.5 Project implementation activities and deliverables

All project activities should be listed here with a description and sub-activities. Significant deliberations should be reflected in annex 5 Implementation timeline. Add rows as needed.

Please number the activities as shown below to indicate association of activities to the related outputs provided above in section E.5. Similarly, please number sub-activities as shown below to associate them to the related output.

#### Component 1: Government, civil society and communities are strengthened to support local resilience to climate change impacts, including by providing access to climate information and weather services

**Output 1.1:** Community-based climate change adaptation and disaster risk reduction mechanisms are strengthened

##### Activity 1.1.1
* Establish new or strengthen existing CDDCCs and build their capacity, including strengthening social inclusion and gender balance.

#### Sub-activities

1.1.1.1 CDDCC develops and field-tests a community-based climate change adaptation and disaster risk reduction mechanism (e.g., through the use of GLA tools) in at least 80% of target communities.
1.1.1.2 Assess CDDCC data using field-tested GLA tools to identify gaps, including social inclusion and gender balance, engage civil society and community members to identify opportunities to strengthen CDDCCs (e.g., through the use of GLA tools) in at least 80% of target communities.
1.1.1.3 Strengthen CDDCC capacity as necessary based on the findings of 1.1.1.2, including by providing training and community workshops.

#### Deliverables

Assessments of CDDCCs in all target communities have new or strengthened CDDCCs.
At least 80% of target communities have new or strengthened CDDCCs.
Social inclusion and gender balance is at least 75% in CDDCCs in all target communities.
Gender balance in CDDCCs is at least 90% in all target communities.
People with disabilities represented on CDDCC in at least 80% of target communities.
Youth representatives on CDDCC in at least 80% of target communities.

## Activity 1.2.2
### Identify key local issues that drive climate vulnerability and barriers to their development of adaptation plans and measure program impacts.
- **Support identification of key challenges and barriers that drive climate vulnerability in order to develop a framework for the development of local adaptation plans.**

#### Subactivities:
- **1.2.2.1:** Build capacity of Area Councils' climate change focal points to conduct baseline profiling and documenting local vulnerabilities (consistent with the Community Adaptation Framework, GEF principles and checklist).
- **1.2.2.2:** Conduct baseline household-level vulnerability assessment surveys to document resource dependence and identify adaptation actions.
- **1.2.2.3:** Conduct baseline household-level vulnerability assessment surveys to document resource dependence and identify adaptation actions.

### Activity 1.2.3
#### Development of Inclusive Community Adaptation Plans and identification of key priority climate resilience building actions from the VCORP adaptation package of interventions.
- Facilitate development of Community Adaptation Plans (CAPs) that include climate resilience building actions from the VCORP adaptation package of interventions.

#### Subactivities:

E

**E.7 Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max 500 words, approximately 1 page)**

207. The project has a monitoring, evaluation, accountability and learning (MEAL) framework designed to measure two main areas:

- **Project-level**: Measuring progress against the approved project document, updated NDC and the Adaptation Performance Measurement Framework.
- **Contribution to key GCF Investment Criteria and the Resilience Framework**.

208. Project-level monitoring and evaluation will be undertaken in compliance with Save the Children’s MEL systems and processes as well as aligning with the GCF’s Monitoring, Evaluation, Accountability and National Monitoring and Evaluation Policy (MEP) to ensure complementarity with existing government systems and reporting processes.

209. The PMU’s MEAL manager/lead will be responsible for designing a study to establish baseline, mid-line and final data for:
- **Project-level core indicators and outcomes**
- **Programmatic results and indicators**

**Monitoring**

Setting up the monitoring system of the project will involve different steps. The primary responsibility for day-to-day project monitoring and implementation is with the Project Team. The PMU will provide technical support to the Project Team in order to ensure the efficient management of the project. A project inception workshop, involving the Save the Children Australia, the Co-Executing Entity (CSE), and other relevant stakeholders, will be held at the beginning of the project cycle. The purpose of this project inception workshop is to:
a) assist the Project Team and stakeholders to understand and take ownership of the project strategy, objectives and outcomes;
b) provide an opportunity for the Project Team to present their project plan and discuss the complementary responsibilities of the project team and the national government ministries including reporting and communication lines and conflict resolution mechanisms; c) review the results framework, re-assess baselines as needed, and discuss reporting, monitoring, and evaluation arrangements.

211. The Project Team will ensure that the indicators included in the project results framework are monitored and reported on annually and will report progress against these indicators to the PMU. The PMU will provide technical support to the Project Team as it monitors project results and overall GCF goals. The Project M&E will cover each two levels of performance: GCF-level performance (projected performance against investment criteria) and project-level performance.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V1.0 | PAGE 91

## Evaluation

### Mid-term evaluation process

213. The project's mid-term evaluation process will include an internal impact evaluation and an independent process evaluation. An independent final impact evaluation will take place no later than three months prior to operational closure of the project.

The mid-term evaluation will assess the extent to which the project has achieved its intended outcomes, with a focus on the performance and impact of the project's completed activities, and will include assessment against OECD-DAC and GCF evaluation criteria. These may include the following: financial sustainability; institutional capacity building; gender equity and inclusiveness; innovation and potential for paradigm shift; country ownership; coherence of climate finance; and learning by reviewing lessons learned.

The mid-term evaluation should also assess whether there is sufficient evidence to project implementers, beneficiaries, and stakeholders (including donors) to support evidence-based decision-making. The mid-term evaluation will be used as a basis for the development of an action plan that may include corrective measures or adjustments to the project design, including out any necessary corrective measures for the remaining period of the project. The final evaluation will assess the relevance of the intervention and its contribution towards achieving the Fund's higher-level goal of achieving a paradigm shift in climate finance.

215. The full MEAL plan is available in Annex 11.

# RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## F.1. Mitigation measures (max. 3 pages)

* The top 5 risks for the project are provided below with further details on all potential risks (including low risks) in Annex 2.

### Selected Risk Factor 1: Financial Management

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Low | Medium |

#### Description
Public financial management systems of the government's Executing Entity (MoCEG) and government ministries implementing activities are unable to absorb, manage, and distribute the increased volume of financing from this project on top of existing budget expenditure.

#### Mitigation Measure(s)
The fiduciary management procedures that will be utilized for the project under the Vanuatu Government decentralized model significantly reduce risk with the intent of the Vanessa PFEM Act (see Section G3 for further details). In addition, the AE has established and effective corporate policies related to financial management and reporting.

While the Government does not have access to sufficient finance to meet current adaptation needs. Vanuatu has the Pacific region's only functional Government Financial Management Information System. The system is fully integrated with the Ministry of Finance and Treasury (MoF) and all government accounts. This means that all government ministries currently access all of their funds via the one system for budgeting, accounting, and reporting purposes.

The project will be implemented through a decentralized PFEM model which has been developed by MoF that has enabled Vanuatu to be the first and, in many cases, only recipient of direct budget support from a wide range of donors including DFAT, the European Union, the US NRC, USAID, UNICEF, and others. The Vanessa PFEM Act will provide for the establishment of a decentralized PFEM model which provides for decentralized coverage through Government agencies in the islands (including Department of Finance). This means that all government ministries will be able to access their funds via one system. All government ministries (including Department of Local Authorities and Provincial governments; Department of Agriculture, Department of Fisheries; Department of Climate Change; and National Disaster Management Office) will also be tracked and reported via PFEM.

### Selected Risk Factor 2: Long-term Capacity Building

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Other | Medium | Medium |

#### Description
Many international and regional donors are leaving projects in Vanuatu that include site-specific activities and discrete government positions. Once this funding ends, experience has shown that there isn't the financial capacity for government to continue the role and the staff and institutional knowledge is lost, and project activities cease.

#### Mitigation Measure(s)
The VCCP/IP will mitigate this by embedding sustainable approaches to changing the governance framework and empowering government ministries to increasingly lead implementation of project activities. This will ensure that the project's benefits are sustained beyond the life of the project and even after the project ends. A key focus of the project is in building local capacity to access and effectively utilize future flows of climate finance, including at the most local level (by supporting the development of

## Selected Risk Factor 3: Prolonged period to establish the project

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | High | Medium |

### Description
A lack of technical capacity and slow government systems and processes can affect mobilization and implementation of projects, leading to significant delays in delivery of work plans and meeting funding agreement obligations, particularly in year 1.

### Mitigation Measures
The implementation schedule and budget allocations have specifically taken this risk into account, and the project has been designed to minimize absorptive capacity. Early project activities (particularly in years 1 and 2) will be significantly supported by Save the Children Vanuatu which can mobilize quickly to support government activities while recruiting training and other capacity building tasks are being undertaken.

## Selected Risk Factor 4: Limited Government Capacity

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | High | Medium |

### Description
Current limitations in technical capacity and available resources within government, with multiple competing demands on personnel, can undermine robust governance structures for selecting new personnel, planning, and administration of project work plans.

### Mitigation Measures
The project will be designed to avoid burdening any one government delivery partner to minimize absorptive capacity and creating capacity constraints that delay project implementation. This is a key risk identified in the Government Capacity Assessment (GCA) undertaken by Save the Children departments, including Department of Local Authorities and Provincial governments; Department of Agriculture and Livestock; Department of Education and Training; Department of Health; Department of Planning and Environment; Department of Rural Development and Land Management; Department of Social Services. The Project Office will ensure no one department creates implementation bottlenecks as has been the experience with similar projects in Vanuatu, where Save the Children has worked closely with all relevant government departments and other delivery partners supported and through Save the Children's systems and national footprint. The breadth of the project will also help ensure country capacity is not overwhelmed. Working across multiple sectors and levels of government will allow for a more balanced approach to capacity building and considered development of in-country, local and ongoing technical capacity and ownership supported by robust governance structures. This will be achieved through close engagement against government and the building of government skills and technical capacity throughout the life of the project.

### G.1 Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

#### 216. The proposal is in Category C in terms of environmental and social risks. ESS screening of the proposed project was undertaken against the GCF Guidelines for the Environmental and Social Considerations and Standards (GCS) as well as the Project’s own Environmental and Social Management Plan phase.

The SAP ESS screening is substantially the same as the Program Environmental and Social Sustainability Management System (PESMSM) that Save the Children uses internally for all projects. The PESMSM has been designed to ensure that the project can be implemented in a manner that addresses times for micro-and small-scale activities that promote and support certain scalable and transformative interventions, such as those related to climate change adaptation and mitigation.

The SAP ESS screening is based on the GCF Guidelines for Environmental and Social Considerations and Standards (GCS) as well as the Project’s own Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP). The project will use the SAP guidelines because the proposed activities are small-scale at the individual activity level, including soil conservation, water harvesting, and community forest management activities.

#### 217. If undetected or inadequately managed, the environmental and social risks associated with these activities could have direct, indirect, and long-term cumulative impacts and have taken into account the proposed activities area of influence. SEAH provisions have been included in the project risk screening and its Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP) to ensure that the project will be implemented in a manner that prevents and respond effectively to SEAH in a survivor-centered and gender-responsive way in accordance with the Green Climate Fund’s Guidelines for Environmental and Social Considerations and Standards (GCS) (BIM-2021/8).

The SAP ESS screening has been carried out at the project level. The screening process enables the most serious potential impacts of the activities to be considered. G.2. Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The project will be implemented in accordance with Vanuatu’s gender equality policies. Access to resources, decision making, information and safety are all mediated through norms associated with gender, disability and age. In rural communities, agriculture and fisheries are family enterprises with different roles for men and women. Women have a greater role in the household economy than the majority of men; however, women's access is based on fathers, husbands and sons. Decision-making positions within households are also determined by age and gender. The eldest male has authority over all other members of his household, including children and wives. At family level there is greatest evidence of shared decision-making. Community development, church activities and education are dominated by men who are the primary income earners, decision makers and are starting to shift social norms. People with disability and children remain excluded from these decisions.

22 National agriculture and fisheries policies now recognize the value of and importance of engaging whole communities in sustainable livelihoods. However, women's access is limited for people with disabilities. However long-established practices of working with men have led to gender bias, focusing on their roles in agriculture and fisheries are one way to change. Women’s roles in ensuring food security and nutrition are often overlooked or undervalued. The project will support the development of community change adaptations. Yet women's ability to grow food, fish, collect marine resources, and access

Comprehensive community and stakeholder engagement is at the heart of this project on which locally-led adaptation is built. Underpinning a stable governance environment will lie a focus on equity at its heart. COCOVI will aim for gender equity in representation and decision making, and Community Based Adaptation (CBA) processes will be used to ensure that women’s voices are heard.

The project will build from all community perspectives. The project will ensure that women and men have equal voice in the planning of adaptation activities and that women participate equally with men in all CBA activities.
