## Financial management and procurement (max. 500 words, approximate page 1)

### Fiduciary arrangements will follow a decentralized model that involves the Accredited Entity (AE), the Ministry of Finance and the Union Government as well as other relevant entities such as Executive Entities, the Ministry of Climate Change and Save the Children Vanuatu.

Following endorsement of the project by the National Parliament, the AE will be established. The AE will be funded directly by the Vanuatu National Government from the AE via the Reserve Bank of Vanuatu, a decentralized financial institution that can be utilized, where each separate government agency is responsible for its own budget / appropriation following approval by the National Parliament and reporting to its Project Management Unit at the Ministry of Climate Change for all expenditure. Existing administrative arrangements will continue to apply with the exception of the removal of the existing line ministry structures located in the Corporate Service Units of each ministry (as opposed to being centralized within the AE). The decentralized model is consistent with the Union Government’s Budget Support, as it significantly reduces the overall administration costs of the program and aligns with the Union Government’s commitment to devolve the AE roles and functions. This requirement will be stipulated in the sub agreements.


---
**Page 100**
---

229. Fundholding by Save the Children Vanuatu would be used for implementation of its work plan and programming, including the engagement of procured parties, as approved by the Project Steering Committee.

### Transparency Implications

* A decentralized model allows for coding of project activities at a higher level in the chart of accounts and reporting on them through general reporting would be able to produce the financial reports required without additional analysis and manipulation.
* The transparency implications are that the funds used for project spending as expenses will be shown alongside other sector spending, which increases transparency and understanding of how these funds are being utilized compared to funds from other donors and the Government budget. The funds will also be subject to the same level of scrutiny by the public as other public monies who can view and manage the budget used for the project activities they are expected to deliver.

### Administration Implications

* A decentralized approach may be more administratively cumbersome as it entails multiple spending units across the Government, however these units already exist within the Corporate Service Units of each Ministry. The only additional administrative burden would be the need to establish a separate administration and the workload would be distributed across pre-existing line ministry structures. This will also allow for the use of existing systems such as the Australian Aid/UNDAWN Budget Support so it significantly reduces the overall administration costs of the project.

### Cost Implications

* As mentioned above, a decentralized model allows more efficient coding of the project activities in terms of their alignment with the overall 'funds control' of the Government system. By spreading the funds across the various spending units within each Ministry (based on the project's purpose, activity, and cost centre) for which there are strong financial controls already in place that would prevent any misuse or misappropriation.

### Alignment with government and donor policies

* A decentralized model is aligned with both Government and donor policies and allows for an accurate representation of spending by sector and such reports could be produced using the pre-existing government reporting tools and templates without the need to extract and manipulate data externally. This will also allow for a more transparent and accountable reporting system that aligns donor and Government resources in terms of how they are operationalized and reported on by sector.

### Potential Drawbacks

* A decentralized model enables the project to link seamlessly into the Vanuatu Government's system of performance management and reporting. Under program budgeting, each agency of Government is required to submit a program budget that includes an indicative statement of expected outputs and outcomes for the year. The program budget also provides information on the financial resources in the presentation of the budget to the Parliament, and importantly contains service delivery or output and outcome data. This will allow for the project's performance management reporting system to be linked into a decentralized financial model to tie into the performance management reporting of Government, thereby providing greater transparency.

* Annual audit of the financials of the project will be conducted by SCA’s independent external auditor PriceWaterhouseCoopers. The annual audit review is to be conducted by SCA’s in-house Head of Internal Audit for years 4 and 5 which will focus on compliance with Australian accounting procedures and GCF requirements. The Australian accounting standards which are aligned to IFRS will be used.

# Secretariat’s assessment of FP184

## Proposal name: Vanuatu community-based climate resilience project (VCCRP)

### Accredited entity: Save the Children Australia

### Country: Vanuatu

### Project/programme size: Small

## I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

   **Strengths** | **Points of caution**
   ------------- | -------------
   None          | None
   None          | None

2. The Board shall wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and conditions as in the annexure sheet and addendum VII, titled "List of proposed conditions and recommendations."

## II. Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment

### 2.1 Project background

3. Vanuatu is one of the most vulnerable countries in the world to extreme weather events induced by climate change. The country has a high vulnerability index (0.75) for natural disasters, and received the highest disaster risk score of 49.7 in the World Risk Report in 2020.

4. The Vanuatu Community-Based Climate Resilience Project is designed to fill the adaptation deficit in urban and rural communities across six provinces. It will do so by supporting access to and utilization of local-level climate information, integrating climate risks into community planning processes, and building capacity for disaster risk reduction. The project will directly benefit 90,157 people in 28 Area Councils. These are the most vulnerable and socially marginalized people in Vanuatu, who will benefit from the implementation of the project.


---
**Page 105**
---

### Component-by-component analysis

#### Component 1: Government, civil society and communities are strengthened to adopt local resilience strategies in response to climate change through access to climate information and early warning (total cost USD 6,480 million; GCF cost USD 6,230 million or 97% cent)

- **Component 1 aims to strengthen the resilience of 254 local communities (90 per cent of the total number of communities in Vansana) through the establishment and strengthening of Community Disaster and Climate Change Committees (CDCCCs) that will guide adaptation and disaster preparedness activities at community level. Under this component, Community Adaptation Plans will be developed with CDCCCs and awareness-raised on communities’ climate change risks through targeted information, education and communication materials. It will extend the capacity of CDCCCs to manage climate change risks in their communities using various available techniques, which will increase the technical competency of the communities in utilizing climate information services for disaster risk management.**

- **Baseline assessment shows that CDCCCs are either non-existent or not fully functional in the target communities. However, as a result of the active engagement and strong determination by the government to establish and strengthen these committees at all levels of reference, including their structure, roles and responsibilities, have already been defined within the National Disaster Management Plan (NDMP) and the Climate Change Action Plan (CCAP). The proposed activity of strengthening CDCCCs is therefore deemed to be appropriate in filling the financial gap for the establishment and strengthening of these committees at all levels and enabling community-level ownership in managing disaster risks. Although the Government of Vansana’s in-kind contribution will be used to establish and strengthen the committees, it is important to secure resources to continue the committee activities after project completion.**

- **The activities related to climate information services and early warning systems have been considered as a separate component (component 2) because they are not directly linked with the potential of proposed activities within those of approved GCF project PV051, “Climate Information Services for Disaster Risk Management Planning in Vansana” (Van-CS-RDP). Detailed mapping analysis has been conducted at the national level and the data alignment is not aligned between the two projects. As essence, output 1.3 in the proposed project will enable wider dissemination of climate information services to all communities within the country as well as the community level. This includes utilizing outputs from the Van-CS-RDP project as an input to the proposed project. The same applies for early warning systems which are included in the Van-CS-RDP project’s climate information services on an ongoing basis.**

- **The appropriateness and necessity of the proposed component has been justified in that the main objective of this component is to provide services under one umbrella that covers all types of beneficiaries, including government (Area Councils), civil society and communities. Activities will be implemented at community level through the established committees as per component 3, especially the knowledge generation and dissemination for the target communities.**


---
**Page 106**
---

### Component 2: Sustainable, locally appropriate actions are implemented to meet community adaptation needs to create climate-resilient, sustainable development pathways (total cost USD 145.6 million; GCF cost USD 138.9 million, or 95 per cent)

- **Component 2.1**: Will strengthen livelihoods and fisheries management through nature-based solutions in the agriculture and fisheries sectors. This will include the rehabilitation and restoration of upland forest areas and shoreline vegetation, as well as the protection of critical natural resources such as mangroves and coral reefs. The project will also support sustainable land management. This will be done through distributing 500,000 tree seedlings and building at least 254 erosion management structures.

- **Component 2.2**: Will support communities in carrying out adaptation measures in household plots through training and the provision of seeds and seedlings.

### The proposed component is designed to address a range of adaptation activities in the agriculture and fisheries sectors. Target communities will be able to select the most appropriate effective options within the package to meet their needs on the ground. Given this, the project has adopted an adaptive management approach. This means that the overall design is deemed adequate and the most feasible approach in delivering tailored adaptation solutions for each community. The specific adaptation measures to be implemented have been determined for each community at the project appraisal stage; the proposal still manages to set several key performance indicators (KPIs) and targets, which are consistent with the overall design based on wide stakeholder consultations and the targets may change depending on the context.

- **Under this component**, GCF grants will be used to generate tangible outcomes to address food insecurity in the agriculture and fisheries sectors. The component focuses on the supply side of agricultural production and the development of new technologies and techniques that would incentivize target beneficiaries to adopt such inputs and techniques are required. As the project is still at an early stage, it has not yet been possible to identify specific mechanisms and business models have not been incorporated into the design of the project.

- **The proposed agriculture and water conservation techniques are well-known and recognized by local communities as effective in addressing climate change impacts. Techniques are based on a comprehensive stocktake of existing and traditional agricultural practices and crop calendars. As for the requested GCF grant funding, the proposal provided sound justification for the request and the expected financial return is estimated at 10 per cent with an internal rate of return and net present value.

### Component 2.3: The project’s climate-resilience is enhanced by building adaptive governance systems at the local level and enhancing local provincial national linkages (total cost USD 85.1 million; GCF cost USD 84.7 million, or 94 per cent)

- **Component 2.3.1**: Will support the integration of provincial and national actors as well as local authorities to integrate climate risks into their governance planning and budgeting processes. Based on the findings from the vulnerability assessment, a multi-sectoral disaster risk reduction and social protection system will be established, including a cash transfer system, to protect the most vulnerable population against climate change. The second part of this component is to generate and disseminate information about climate risks and adaptation measures in order to increase awareness gleaned through this project.

- **Component 2.3.2**: A financial exit strategy is provided only to a limited extent. The proposed component offers a good opportunity to design and maintain a financial exit strategy which is viable in the local context and the context of projected climate impacts, supported by a cash transfer system and business plans.


---
**Page 107**
---

### III. Assessment of performance against investment criteria

#### 3.1 Impact potential Scale: N/A

- The project targets the most vulnerable and marginalized communities in Vanuatu covering a vast area of islands, including some of the most remote islands in the Vanuatu archipelago.
- These communities will be better able to adapt to climate change impacts, which are expected to increase over time. As a result, the targeted communities will become more resilient and achieve diversified livelihoods through the sustainable use of natural resources and improved access to markets for their products fostered by the project.
- The packages and the information services will enable the target communities to diversify their livelihoods in ways that are less vulnerable to shocks and hazards as well as less prone to future shocks caused by cyclones.

#### 3.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: N/A

The Vanuatu community-based resilience project fosters innovative solutions to meet the current climate challenges faced by communities on a daily basis and empowers them to become less vulnerable and build a solid platform to further diversify their livelihoods and adapt to future climate change impacts.

- By combining the adaptation packages, well designed and adjusted to each of the provinces, the project will provide a unique platform for the targeted communities. With detailed and continuous information and built capacity across all areas of Vanuatu society, the project will be able to improve the current situation for the poorest and most vulnerable populations in the country.
- The project will also foster rural entrepreneurship by empowering communities across the archipelago in adapting to future challenges and shocks through improved livelihood systems to provide healthy and nutritious food as well as fostering rural entrepreneurship.

The adaptation packages offer a unique platform to cater to the demands, challenges, opportunities, and constraints of each community. This is based on its local context and open systems.


---
**Page 108**
---

3.3 Sustainable development potential

The project empowers communities through capacity building, ensuring better access to education and training opportunities for youth, improving transport services and enabling communities to diversify their food systems and create jobs and growth at the local level as well through the adaptation packages. This will allow communities to strengthen and diversify their household livelihoods in a changing climate.

The project will further boost health conditions for targeted communities through better access to clean water and diversified diets with locally produced and processed food, and this access and processing skills will be sustained over time so that they take a leading role in knowledge implementation and beyond.

Furthermore, the project fosters stronger gender mainstreaming across the activities, ensuring equal participation of women in decision-making across the community groups and at the provincial level.


3.4 Needs of the recipient

The project targets the most needy, vulnerable and poorest communities across the archipelago. The targeted communities are also the most marginalized.

Communities face a range of challenges including: 1) economic vulnerability; 2) social economies across the six provinces, with market access blocked to the local communities, not to mention the collapse of the tourism sector;

3) Vulnerability that experiences category 5 tropical cyclones, causing significant damage to local infrastructure and food systems and presenting a challenge to the government in financing their rehabilitation and development.

The project will build the government of areas across all levels and in parallel with empowering communities to better withstand such shocks and hazards and boost local food systems to be more productive, resilient and long term oriented.


3.5 Country ownership

The project objectives and structure are fully aligned with all strategic frameworks of the country.


3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

### Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### Environmental and social safeguards

The project is classified as environmental and social safeguard category C. The project has developed a comprehensive environmental and social management plan that provides for screening of proposed activities and management of potential minimal environmental and social residual risks and impacts. The environmental and social co-benefits include improved access to education, health services, and financial inclusion through reforestation, erosion control and shoreline protection; and increased access to educational opportunities and income generation for women and youth in coastal communities through better planning and implementation of disaster risk reduction strategies. The AE has requested minor revisions and clarifications of the plan, which will need to be addressed prior to Board circulation.

The revised GCF Environmental and Social Policy adopted by decision BIM-2021/18 requires that projects identify and manage SEAR-related potential risks and impacts in their relevant safeguards instruments, such as the Residual Risk Management Plan. As SEAR-related risks are associated with the uneven power dynamics within communities and the lack of transparency and accountability, the project examines such risks with regard to the project’s workers and affected community members. The project recognizes that some community constituents spending on expected outcomes, including occasionally unwilling, will have influence or relative power over the distribution of project inputs where community members may not be aware of or able to hold project staff accountable.

To resolve the SEAR risks associated with the uneven power dynamics, the project has identified relevant measures in the Residual Risk Management Plan. To mitigate and manage the SEAR risks, the project is building awareness and training for both its workforce and affected community on the SEAP policy and SEAR-related procedure for monitoring and reporting of incidents. The project will also develop incident response plans to identify and equip community child protection and SEAR focal points to monitor the conduct of project staff and ensure that they are not engaging in any form of exploitation.

To ensure that the project-specific grievance redress mechanism responds to SEAR incidents, the project will train staff to handle sensitive documents and make referrals to appropriate service providers.

The project identifies SEAR mitigation measures, including integrating SEAP into staff induction training; building awareness on SEAR prevention and response among implementation of community-based activities and monitoring SEAR risks and incidents throughout project implementation. The project recognizes that putting in place prevention and response measures into place will not imply additional costs to the project as they are seen or costed as part of the project design.


---
**Page 110**
---

### Gender policy

The AE provided a gender assessment and action plan with the funding proposal and therefore complies with the requirements of the GCF Gender Policy.

- Women have equal rights to freely choose discrimination on the basis of sex.
- The country prepared the second national gender equality policy to enhance women's economic empowerment, including access to land, credit, and other productive resources; women's participation in leadership and decision-making and foster gender-responsive community-driven development initiatives.
- The country has signed the Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action and the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women. In addition, it has signed related regional commitments such as the Pacific Leaders' Gender Equality Declaration.
- National authorities are responsible for policy formulation and implementation in collaboration with the Ministry of Justice and Community Services. The department is responsible for policy and programme development and implementation at national level; however, it has limited technical and financial capacity at the national and provincial level to deliver on gender equality.
- Social norms shape gender matters by giving men control over women's behaviour and privileges to make decisions in traditional settings. Women have limited access to resources such as land, credit, and other productive resources.
- Women are active participants and producers in the fisheries and agriculture sectors. They engage in fishing activities, including small-scale commercial fishing. Women lead in weeding, planting and maintaining crops closer to home; while men take on clearing and ploughing of fields and other tasks that require more physical strength. Women participate in training and extension initiatives.
- Extension services and enterprise development training usually target male farmers. Women are involved in aquaculture activities such as deep water fishing with larger vessels for sale or a male-dominated activity, while women undertake coastal grazing and fishing from canoes for food security, and aquaculture and fish processing to generate income.
- The gender assessment also describes challenges faced by people with disabilities and the compounded effects of climate change on their lives. Women are targeted in stakeholder engagement, training and inclusion in committees.
- Community meetings, key informant interviews and focus group discussions undertaken as part of the gender assessment highlight that while women face challenges associated with adapting to climate change and responding to natural disasters, the limited inclusion of women in community decision-making is a major barrier to their empowerment. Women are particularly highlighted, especially by women; training on agricultural practices, value addition and financial literacy.
- The gender assessment concludes that while the project has made some progress towards addressing the gender equality issues identified during the baseline analysis, the gender assessment does not clearly describe the opportunities presented by the project to address them. Nonetheless, they are reflected in the gender action plan.

The project aims to include women in community-based activities aligned with those of the project, performance indicators, baseline sex-disaggregated targets, timelines and responsibilities.


---
**Page 111**
---

structures by targeting a gender balance in CCOGs to improve women’s participation in leadership and decision-making positions at the community level. Project benefits will be accessible to both women and men and sex-disaggregated targets are included for each activity. Although the project is not specifically designed as an adaptation project, the gender action plan describes them broadly as project-provided resources which will be accessed by women and men in the same proportion. The project will provide training for women and men to participate in training on climate-resilient agriculture, canoe making and value addition skills. This responds to issues raised in stakeholder engagement during the project conceptualization phase that highlighted a lack of access to agricultural inputs such as the form of seeds, seedlings, simple agricultural tools, food processing and preservation technologies, and storage facilities for perishable goods. The provision of these resources and preservation equipment such as solar food dryers and freezers to support food security needs and provide additional income opportunities will be included in the action plan. Opportunities to enhance resilience to climate change are reflected in the gender action plan through the support provided by the project to women’s groups and youth groups which will be established for bringing agricultural and fisheries goods and value addition.

### Implementation arrangements include a gender equality and social inclusion adviser for the project, as well as a gender focal point at the national level. This will ensure that the execution of the action plan. The Department of Women's Affairs will be included in the project steering committee to provide guidance on gender equality and social inclusion aspects of all the activities in the action plan. In addition, costs allocated from the project’s budget are included for each activity listed in the action plan.

The AE is required to collect sex-disaggregated data on all activities where communities benefit from project-provided resources and report according to GCF as per the action plan.


## Risks

### Overall programme assessment (medium risk)

This is an adaptation project that seeks to support vulnerable rural communities in Vanuatu by implementing targeted adaptation actions and facilitating decentralized governance structures. The project will also provide training on climate change trends and disaster risk. GCF is requested to provide USD 26.3 million in grants. Additional co-financing will come from the government of Australia (A$10 million in cash contributions, A$5.5 million in kind contributions) and the AE ($8 million grant for project management costs).

Vanuatu is a small island developing state. Moreover, given the public nature of the proposed activities, there may be some resistance for community-based adaptation; the AE deems it appropriate to request grant funding.


### Accredited entity/executing entity's capability to execute the current programme (medium risk)

SCA is the AE of this project. It is a non-governmental organization based in Australia that operates internationally through its network. Globally, SCA is implementing a portfolio of over 100 resilience-related projects and programmes valued at more than USD 280 million, including climate change adaptation activities. The organization has experience in areas such as increasing adaptive capacity and speed recovery from shocks and stresses. Specifically, SCA has implemented projects in Vanuatu that are relevant to the proposed project type and region of Vanuatu.


---
**Page 112**
---

## Project-specific execution risks (medium risk)

### Long-term sustainability: two of the three major components in this project will be dedicated to long-term sustainability. The third component is designed to foster understanding of climate change impacts and develop local adaptation plans, strengthen the capacity of government officials and incorporate climate risk analysis and financing strategies into the planning process. This will ensure that communities are better prepared for concrete actions within communities. The lag may affect the long-term sustainability of the project.

### Financial resources: there is a lack of certainty about how much financial resources from the national government will be allocated to community-based adaptation activities. However, the national government does not currently have sufficient funding to support these activities and has not prioritized them at the national level; little to no funding is invested in community-based adaptation as this is not featured in the planning process. The project proposes that funds will be provided through the central allocation of funds, subsequent funding to ensure the sustainability of community resilience projects.

### Economic analysis: a cost-benefit analysis returns a project-level economic internal rate of return of -1 per cent. The benefits are estimated by valuing the avoided damage, erosion and carbon emissions from deforestation and degradation as well as the avoided costs associated with climate change adaptation activities. The negative internal rate of return indicates that the total costs outweigh the envisaged benefits, which may give rise to questions about the viability and effectiveness of GCF investment in this project.

## GCF portfolio concentration risk (low risk)

### In the case of approval, the impact of this proposal on the GCF portfolio risk remains non-material and within the risk appetite in terms of concentration level, results area or single proposal.

## Compliance risk (medium risk)

### Vanuatu is not the subject of any current targeted United Nations Security Council resolutions or sanctions regimes. SCA, as the AE, confirms that no project activities will be undertaken in a jurisdiction which has been identified by the UN as being subject to United Nations Security Council resolutions. Furthermore, no individual or entity that is listed on any United Nations Security Council sanctions list, including the United States Sanctions List, is associated with this project or its activities, either as a counterparty, implementer or beneficiary.

### Legal assessment

77. The Accreditation Master Agreement was signed with the Accredited Entity on 20 December 2019 (the "AMT"), and it became effective on 20 May 2020.

78. The Accredited Entity has provided a legal opinion of its General Counsel dated 12 August 2020, which states that the GCF has been granted appropriate privileges and authority to implement the project.

79. The proposed project will be implemented in Vanuatu. The GCF has signed a bilateral agreement with the Government of Vanuatu; however, such agreement is still pending its entry into force. This means that the privileges and immunities are only effectively conferred upon the GCF when it enters into force. There may also be jurisdictional issues amongst other things, if GCF is not protected against litigation or expropriation in this country, which risks undermining the project.

80. The Heads of the Independent Redress Mechanism (IRM) and independent Integrity Unit (IUU) have both expressed that it would not be legally feasible to undertake their redress activities with respect to any claims made by a party under the GCF against Vanuatu or relevant privileges and immunities. Therefore, it is recommended that disbursements by the GCF are subject to appropriate legal protections in relation to jurisdictional protection against litigation and expropriation in the country, or has been provided with appropriate privileges and immunities.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP184

## Proposal name: Vanuatu community-based climate resilience project (VCCRP)

### Accredited entity: Save the Children Australia

### Country/ies: Vanuatu

### Project/programme size: Small

## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1. Impact potential Scale: N/A

This funding proposal for a small project in environmental and social safeguards category C is submitted by Vanuatu, a small island developing State* in the Pacific, with 65 inhabited islands and approximately 280,000 inhabitants. The project is implemented in partnership with Save the Children Australia as the accredited entity (AE) and two co-executing entities - the Government of Vanuatu and the Department for International Development (DFID). The total cost of the funding proposal is USD 32.65 million, as detailed in paragraph 29 below.

1. Current available evidence, combining observed historical trends with future projections, indicates that climate change will have significant impacts on communities in Vanuatu. Projected future trends in three key climatic parameters are: (1) temperature and air humidity; (2) rainfall patterns (increased duration of dry periods and changing frequency and intensity of extreme rainfall and rainfall associated with Nino/Southern Oscillation events); and 3) tropical cyclones (increased number, increased intensity, and more extensive landfall). Category 5 cyclones in 2015 and 2019 caused loss of life and extensive damage to infrastructure. The frequency of category 4 and 5 cyclones over the past 10-year period is 101 tropical cyclones developed or crossed into the Vanuatu Exclusive Economic Zone (EEZ) during this time, with a significant increase in the number of intense cyclone intensity.

2. With about 80 per cent of the population of this Pacific island nation dependent on subsistence agriculture, climate change poses a significant risk to Vanuatu's agriculture sector and food security. Agricultural activities in Vanuatu are particularly susceptible to climate change impacts due to their reliance on rainfall for irrigation, increased frequency of droughts, heatwaves, salinization processes, increases in evapotranspiration, seasonal variations and reduction in soil moisture content. Under the RCP 8.5 scenario, soil moisture is expected to decrease significantly, with potential increased evapotranspiration, indicating significant stress on non-irrigated crops. These climate impacts are exacerbated by prolonged dry periods and intense rainfall events that can lead to environmental degradation. Prolonged and intense rainfall damages seedlings and encourages conditions that promote the spread of weeds and pests. The frequency and intensity of extreme heatwaves or temperature increases is likely to reach the maximum heat tolerance thresholds of some staple crops such as taro, yam and upa (sweet potato), especially in traditional crops such as


---
**Page 118**
---

canaveral and yam. Communities on Torres Islands, South Santo, South Malakula, Central Penama, Epi, Erromango, Aniva and Aneityum, in particular, have reported impacts from increased temperatures and declines on cropping yields and lower livestock productivity. These communities are also more vulnerable to climate change than others specifically to food security, with 60 per cent of rural households engaging in coastal fisheries activities, which, along with the loss of mangroves and coral reefs, are increasingly threatened by habitat degradation from pollution, are further threatened by climate change. Changes in ocean temperatures and acidification also have a direct impact on the reproductive success of species in Vanuatu by altering their metabolic rates, growth, reproduction and survival, resulting in changes to species abundance, distribution and sizes that may reduce catches and food security.

In response to these challenges, the project will work with communities to strengthen resilience in the agriculture and fisheries sectors in response to their threats; more comprehensive, integrated adaptation measures are required. Limited focus on climate change has been strengthened by early warning systems for cyclones and both high and sudden onset disasters intensified by climate change. The project's goal statement is that if vulnerable remote coastal communities of Vanuatu have access to improved climate information, integrate climate risks into community planning processes and are supported in implementing adaptation measures, they will be better able to adapt to the impacts of increasing climate variability, extremes and change on their food security and livelihoods.

BEGINING 2013, THE PROJECT WILL WORK WITH COMMUNITIES TO IMPROVE THEIR CAPACITY TO INCREASE CLIMATE RESILIENCE. THROUGH THESE EFFORTS, IT IS EXPECTED THAT THE NUMBER OF HOUSEHOLDS WITH YEAR-ROUND FOOD SECURITY WILL INCREASE. THE PROJECT WILL WORK TO ACHIEVE THIS GOAL VIA ACTIVITIES UNDER THREE INTERLINKED COMPONENTS:

(i) Component 1: Government, civil society and communities are strengthened to support local resilience to climate change impacts, including by providing access to climate information.

(ii) Component 2: Scalable, locally appropriate actions are implemented to meet community adaptation needs to create climate-resilient, sustainable development pathways (adaptation).

(iii) Component 3: Institutional adaptive capacity is enhanced by building adaptive governance systems at the local level and enhancing local provincial-national linkages.

### Adaptation

The project will work with communities in six provinces of Vanuatu to strengthen their ability to increase climate-resilient sustainable development in a number of ways - strengthening adaptive capacity and reducing exposure of vulnerable communities to climate risks; increasing generation, dissemination and use of improved climate information; and building institutional and regulatory systems for climate-responsive planning and disaster risk reduction. Component 1 involves the provision of improved climate information services that will increase diversified/enhanced livelihoods resilient to the conditions of flooding, storm surges, administrative capacity and access to climate change information. Components 2 and 3 will reduce the vulnerability of farmers and fishers through the use of improved climate information services and by building adaptive governance systems at local level. The Climate Change Committee (CCC) for disaster preparedness and response, as well as through the adoption of resilient food preservation and processing techniques and income diversification.

### 1.2 Paradigm shift potential

The project aims to bring about a paradigm shift away from an agricultural system that is highly vulnerable to climate change to climate-resilient food productive systems which ensure food and water security for farmers and others. Achieving reduced vulnerability and increased resilience requires a fundamental transformation of the way adaptation actions are conceived, planned, implemented, monitored, evaluated, and scaled up. The project aims to achieve a transformation in the way adaptation actions are conceived and implemented, providing a model that can be replicated across the country. This will enable communities to make informed decisions about the nature, timing and type of adaptation actions they want to take; and resourcing them to follow through and feed results upward through governance structures at all levels.

The project is designed as an adaptive learning process, with a focus on building capacity for agriculture/flood communities, creating new avenues for remote rural communities to engage with private sector entities and increase the sustainability of project activities. The project will also provide technical assistance to 71 Area Councils and disaster preparedness workshops in early warning systems and Area Council planning and budgeting.

10. The funding proposal identifies as one of three major existing barriers to effective and sustainable locally led adaptation in Vanuatu: "Low incentives for private sector entities to engage with smallholder farmers and other producers to develop and market specifically livelihoods". The project aims to introduce new livelihoods commodities (dried fruits, dried nuts, spices, etc.) into the market. This will be achieved by establishing 90 women-led producer groups to support communities in bringing agricultural and fisheries products to market. These groups are expected to provide a more direct link between farmers and buyers, thus improving access to distant markets for commodities (assumed at no per ton). If these economic activities are successful, the project expects that the private sector will continue to invest, including buyer contracts in place by the end of the project; this would indicate that a significant paradigm shift has taken place whereby the agriculture and fisheries sectors have reached a point where they can be considered as viable livelihoods.

12. This is noted in the project's exit strategy with its six elements, one of which is to "Foster sustainable private sector engagement". The expected economic benefits from this element are expected to be generated due to improvements in agricultural and fisheries practices, including through access to improved GIS, as well as significantly reduced losses from excess production and improved market access. This will result in a more stable supply of income to the community with which the operation and maintenance of the installed processing facilities can be sustained over time. The project also notes that by removing the potential of marketing food surpluses, this may lead to autonomous growth without the need for further public sector investment. It is noted that there are no existing contractual arrangements between local producers and private enterprises in the food sector.

13. There are a number of structural constraints, however, to the engagement of domestic private sector entities with smallholder farmers and other producers in Vanuatu and its tourism sector, or cross-country export markets. Like other small Pacific Island countries with limited infrastructure for trade and transport, Vanuatu has no direct access to major markets from major markets, has a small population spread across many islands, with trade balance skewed towards imports, and has limited domestic market demand. The lack of reliable transportation options between the project sites and the main commercial and residential hubs of "Vanuatu" and the poor communication and transport networks within and between islands", and especially in rural areas, are significant constraints to private sector engagement. Given its very small size, dispersion over vast tracts of the Pacific Ocean, and environmental fragility results in very high transportation costs. The project notes that it is difficult for the private sector to assess an investment that the public sector is likely to remain the main source of formal sector employment and driver of economic growth in Vanuatu "for at least the next 10 years or so in the foreseeable future".

14. The independent AE asked the project proponents if it is realistic to engage with private sector entities and/or middlemen with small and unpredictable volumes of relatively low-value produce that must travel long distances to market. In response, the AE noted that "against a challenge of limited infrastructure for trade and transport, there are many examples in other export markets. Many of these examples are in the tourism industry where tourism service providers have been able to engage with local communities to provide services which can support the local economy as part of the experience they are selling". It also noted that "a number of investors and donors have been undertaking research in private sector income and value chain analysis in the past, including in Vanuatu. However, there is a gap between past and present projects is that it is not easy to engage the private sector, for the reasons outlined in this funding proposal". ## Sustainable development potential

### Environmental co-benefits
In terms of the environment, the project is expected to have environmental co-benefits in the form of increased resilience and sustainability of ecosystem services by reducing reliance on vulnerable resources (e.g., fish) as a primary food source. The implementation of sustainable land use and coastal resource management will lead to the implementation of protections for local coastal habitats (including reduced mangrove clearing, limited reef fishing), establishing new or enhancing existing "blue" areas (e.g., marine protected areas), reducing sedimentation in coastal ditches; and rehabilitating key degraded coastal resources (such as mangroves, seagrass meadows and coral reefs). The project will also contribute to the implementation of sustainable resource management plans; establish living barriers; support reforestation of damaged forests and water catchment areas; reduce soil erosion through land rehabilitation and new or strengthens existing community conservation areas.

### Social co-benefits
The project has a number of social co-benefits, including:
- Increasing the resilience of vulnerable communities to climate change by ensuring that the voices of traditionally underrepresented groups (e.g., youth, people with a disability) are heard in climate-related decision-making forums.
- The project aims to increase broad community health


---
**Page 122**
---

### Economic co-benefits.

The project works to increase the household incomes of rural communities through increased production and marketing of climate-resilient commodities. This includes support to 261 communities on utilizing climate-resilient food preservation techniques to prevent post-harvest losses, diversifying into new agricultural/food commodities and value added products that deliver greater income generation potential, and supporting processing. The project will add products with market potential and supporting processing. In collaboration with the Vanuatu Chamber of Commerce and Industry, the project will provide technical assistance to communities in establishing community-level enterprise and enhance access to markets, and where possible, support production enterprises in accessing finance for business development.

### Gender-sensitive development impact.

The project promotes increasing gender equity through a multi-pronged approach. This includes ensuring that women's voices are heard in climate-related decision-making forums and that the gender nature of climate risks is considered at all levels of planning and implementation, including at the community and province level. This includes leadership training targeted at women, as part of capacity building for disaster risk management and disaster risks at the local level and their needs with the National Disaster Management Centre. In particular, the focus will be on: (i) increasing women's access to climate information; (ii) introducing and supporting local solutions for food security; (iii) identifying the foods most suitable for preservation and provision of processing equipment; (iv) diversifying into new agricultural/food commodities and value added products that deliver greater income generation potential, and supporting processing; and (v) support women-led enterprises in accessing private finance to scale up production and marketing of climate-resilient food products.

### The independent TAP welcomes the inclusion in the gender action plan of child care provision during project implementation to facilitate women's full participation in project activities.

**Page 123**

20. Vanuatu has the highest score on the world on the global disaster index—a score of 49.74, indicating a high degree of physical exposure, relative vulnerability and resultant risk of loss of life from natural hazards. This is intensified by the predicted impacts of climate change discussed in this report. The country’s ability to adapt to these impacts will determine its local community resilience to these impacts. Financing this adaptation gap has posed a significant challenge for Vanuatu, which, despite being one of the world’s most disaster-prone countries, is a lower-middle-income status country, remains highly aid dependent. While steadily increasing economic growth and development have improved the overall level of national infrastructure and resilience, gains remain unevenly distributed to the most vulnerable people and communities will require adaptation assistance for the foreseeable future, particularly given the country’s significant exposure to climate change impacts.

21. The COVID-19 pandemic has further exacerbated Vanuatu’s vulnerability. The effects of the coronavirus disease 2019 pandemic, with the collapse of the tourism sector and reduced tax receipts, have had a significant impact on the economy. The economic downturn that followed hit the country in April 2020, pushed the domestic budget into deficit in 2020/21, and is likely to have a negative impact on public services for years to come.

23. Consultations in preparation for the project by the Ministry of Climate Change and Save the Children Vanuatu revealed significant climate-related impacts on agriculture and fisheries, and the overall economy. The country’s vulnerability is further exacerbated because it provides essential fund services to a widely dispersed, largely rural population, and donor programmes are geared towards addressing immediate needs (e.g., food security, nutrition, poverty reduction, health and education). Only a few initiatives focus on climate or disaster resilience in the long term. Vanuatu has limited capacity for adaptation planning, including into production and governance sectors. While the Government of Vanuatu has access to strong technical climate change capacity, the capacity to deliver adaptation assistance to the local level is weak. The project will therefore aim to build the capacity of communities and Local Governments in Vanuatu urgently need scaled up support to address immediate adaptation priorities and to support the integration of climate into local planning processes.

### 1.5 Country ownership

#### Scope: N/A

24. The project is well aligned with Vanuatu’s policy framework, including its first and subsequent National Development Strategies (NDS) under the Paris Agreement, which highlight adaptation to climate change as the country's overriding concern. The focus of the project will be on three of the five identified adaptation priority areas: namely agriculture and food security; community-based marine resource management; and integrated water resource management.

The project is also aligned with Vanuatu’s National Adaptation Plan (NAP) for Climate Change and Disaster Risk Reduction, as well as its National Strategy for Sustainable Development and National Action Plan on Access to Information, Public Participation in Decision-Making and Accessible Funding for Climate Change Adaptation and Disaster Risk Management.

25. Vanuatu's NAPA, Climate Change and Disaster Risk Reduction Policy and GCF country programme are also fully aligned with the country’s National Sustainable Development Plan, the highest level of policy document that sets out a long-term vision for sustainable development as a key sustainable development priority. Policy objectives include: “Enhance traditional agricultural practices to increase food security”; “Promote climate-resilient agriculture”; "Institutionalize climate change and disaster risk governance; and build institutional capacity and awareness on climate change adaptation, disaster risk reduction and early warning systems in planning, preparedness, response and recovery.” Promote and ensure strengthened resilience of communities through the development of local action plans and strategies; and Access available financing for climate change adaptation and disaster risk management”.

26. Projected actions by the Government of Vanuatu on national priorities for climate change adaptation in 2021-2022 include efforts identified under its updated NDC (2020–2030)

### Efficiency and effectiveness

The total project cost is USD 103.5 million, 80 per cent of which is a USD 82.18 million GCF grant contribution. The remaining USD 47.6 million, or 20 per cent of the total project cost, is made up by the Government of Vanuatu (USD 9.5 million), Save the Children Australia (USD 300,000) and the Australian Department of Foreign Affairs and Trade (USD 4.613 million). Government of Vanuatu contributions are in kind and are distributed across the project components as follows: USD 2.78 million for the climate change component; USD 5.9 million for the disaster risk reduction component, which is significant given the country’s financial position as discussed above. The structure of the project at 30 per cent co-financing is considered appropriate and balanced in light of the country’s context, and the small profit margins expected on small-scale farming and fishing, combined with the fact that the project is not a stand-alone project, means that the GCF project is not expected to crowd out potential private sector investments.

The economic and financial analysis conducted for the project examined specific adaptation measures in detail using the adaptation package menu, and found that all four have sold economic internal rates of return (EIRR) and can be justified on economic grounds. Early warning systems communications equipment (EWSCE) of 111 per

### GCF/02/212/A04/01

#### (cont.) nature-based solutions - coastal management (EUR of 12 per cent), climate-resilient agriculture, with tara and rice as examples (EURs of 19 per cent for tara and 27 per cent for rice) and food processing and storage (EUR of 81 per cent).

### Looking at the project overall, however, the economic analysis showed that the project has an EBRD of -3.5 per cent (discount rate used is 6 per cent), with a net present value discount rate used of 5 per cent. This is because of the high non-economically assessable costs (non-investment costs) required to enable the project to be implemented in a sustainable manner, and to ensure that it can deliver benefits in remote and rural communities that are highly vulnerable to the impacts of climate change. The all-suggested restoration techniques are also very costly, with an average cost per hectare ranging from EUR 100 to EUR 250. This is due to the high costs associated with the project’s design and implementation, as well as the high costs of constraints at all levels (government at national, provincial and Area Council levels as well as at local level), including the lack of capacity at the community level to implement the project and institutional capacity to enhance the sustainability and replicability of the project's adaptation investments and catalytic development. Also significant is the high cost of delivering projects in Pacific Island countries and States, which are characterized by their small land area, large land size and dispersion over vast tracks of the Pacific Ocean, such that the technical assistance and travel costs associated with implementing these projects can be very high compared to those for other developing regions.

### Application of best practices techniques is also important to ensure cost-effectiveness of restoration activities and resources. In relation to the restoration of coastal ecosystems (mangroves swamps, seagrass beds and coral reefs), the project budget is fairly limited (EUR 500,000 per hectare). This means that only a small proportion of the total area will be subject to restoration techniques, some extremely costly (particularly those involving diving), may be utilized. The project has therefore adopted a cost-effective approach in terms of restoring coastal ecosystems extensively, for example cutting off mangroves from the freshwater supply providing protection against flooding and erosion. However, this approach does not provide any reflection on various approaches, techniques, and costs, and methods which have proved effective and cost-effective in recent restoration initiatives, including in the Pacific.

### In conclusion, the project has a very limited budget highlighted is only for actual restoration work, and that there is additional budget in other activities such as awareness raising, capacity building, and monitoring and evaluation. The risk and costs. The development of local community adaptation plans during project implementation will include a feasibility assessment of the most appropriate rehabilitation techniques specific to each coastal ecosystem type. This will be based on lessons learned from successful projects in Vanuatu (e.g., coral gardening), as well as elsewhere in the Pacific (e.g. mangrove restoration and afforestation). The project will also ensure that the selected techniques are cost-effective and communities to suit the site conditions and are cost-effective.

### In follow-up discussion, the independent TAP reiterated its concern, indicating that many coastal ecosystems were already beyond the reach of the project's budget, and that it was difficult to assess the potential of the proposed mangrove restoration when no baseline data existed. The TAP also raised concerns about the feasibility of some projects including whether engineering works might be needed to reconnect mangroves swamps to freshwater supplies, and whether there would be sufficient funding for monitoring and evaluation activities, to avoid the scenario in which newly planted mangroves propagules are lost en masse. In response, the project team indicated that the proposed restoration sites were relatively undamaged state and would need smaller investments to maintain their functionality as climate change buffers. A detailed assessment of the ecosystem condition and what is needed to restore functioning will thus need to be the starting point for assessments of potential

### Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

The independent TAP recommends this funding proposal for approval, subject to one condition.

In order to maximize sustainability of the project's investments in equipment and technology, a costed Operations and Maintenance (O&M) Plan should be developed as a condition to first disbursement, as described below. Therefore, this endorsement by the independent TAP is subject to the following conditions:

**Condition 1: Costed O&M Plan**

Prior to the first disbursement of GCF Proceeds for the Funded Activity, the accredited entity shall finalize and submit to the Fund a costed Operations and Maintenance (O&M) Plan for the project, in a form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat.

Vanuatu community-based climate resilience project (VCGRP)

### Impact potential
SCA welcomes ITAP's positive assessment of the project’s impact potential. This reflects the project’s focus on building the resilience of some of the most vulnerable people and communities in Vanuatu, which is a small island developing state (SIDS) with a low per capita income from least developed country status (and whose economy has been substantially impacted by the COVID-19 pandemic). The project will directly reach over 40% of the rural population and set the foundation for long-term change in the way communities manage their natural resources.

### Paradigm shift potential
SCA notes ITAP's assessment of the project’s paradigm shift potential. We note the concerns expressed on the private sector impact and need for an operations and maintenance plan. As ITAP notes, the project’s modest aims for private sector engagement make sense and are realistic given Vanuatu’s current economic context. The project will leverage dispersed disposable income to support enhanced market engagement among targeted communities to help diversify their livelihoods and increase their resilience in the face of climate change impacts. Regarding the project's operation and maintenance, we note the need to develop a costed O&M plan that is aligned with this condition and will ensure it is met prior to first disbursement of project resources.

### Sustainable development potential
SCA welcomes ITAP’s positive assessment of the project’s sustainable development potential, particularly in relation to the focus on addressing gender equity issues and supporting women's economic empowerment through supporting women’s decision-making roles in local disaster risk management activities and promoting resilient livelihoods diversification activities as this is a key focus of the project.

### Needs of the recipient
SCA welcomes ITAP’s positive assessment of the needs of the recipients, reflecting the urgent need for scaled-up adaptation assistance to highly vulnerable rural and rural communities.

# Efficiency and effectiveness

SCA notes ITAP’s assessment of the project’s efficiency and effectiveness. Regarding the project’s cost-effectiveness, SCA notes that the project is expected to deliver economically assessable costs and high cost of delivery content result in a low overall EER. This is offset by the economic benefits of the project, which are expected to be higher than its costs resulting in an overall positive EER. Furthermore, the project responds to the GCF’s objective to build the climate resilience of the most vulnerable communities. The project will also contribute to the achievement of economically assessable activities to ensure the sustainability of project outcomes. We believe this investment in capacity building, its focus on highly climate vulnerable communities and its emphasis on local ownership and co-financing by development entities in delivering community-level results will project result in a highly efficient and effective project.

Regarding the project’s effectiveness, SCA notes that the project will undertake biophysical assessments at the community level prior to any decisions being made on specific adaptation activities related to restoration of coastal ecosystems. These assessments are expected to be conducted with the participation of local communities to ensure high local ownership of resulting activities.

We note ITAP’s emphasis on ‘good practice’ and ‘best practice’ techniques is also important to ensure the effectiveness of the project and co-financing resources’. We agree, however we would highlight here that best practices must also include a focus on the local context. The technical assistance provided by the project will be tailored to the specific needs of each SIDS in their adaptation objectives in a way that will suit the local context. The project is also placing best practice emphasis on capacity building and knowledge sharing, as well as climate adaptation planning processes and putting the principles for locally-led adaptation into practice.

## Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel:

SCA welcomes ITAP’s overall positive assessment of the funding proposal and notes with gratitude the recommendations to improve the project design and implementation.

We note and accept the proposed condition on the development of a control operations and maintenance plan for the project prior to first disbursement.

# Part 1 VCCRP Gender and Social Inclusion Analysis/Assessment

## Introduction

The project aims to improve the adaptive capacities of rural communities and recognize that within communities, agricultural production is a gendered activity and addressing social disadvantages is a precondition to adaptation. This analysis considers the ways in which gender, age, and disability intersect with climate change and disasters in Vanuatu with a focus on rural communities.

The analysis was conducted by the Gender, Social Inclusion and Climate Change (GESI) team of the Project. The core approach is to embed gender equality and social inclusion across the Project activities and operations through a participatory process that involves all stakeholders at different levels using broad whole-logical framework.

### The six dimensions of inclusion are:

* Law, policy and institutions
* Social norms and beliefs
* Roles, responsibilities and time use.
* Decision-making authority and participation.
* Access to and control of resources; and
* Safety, dignity and well-being.

The analysis was action oriented based on literature review, key informant interviews with national government officials and community profiles in North Ambrym, Epi and North West Santo, undertaken through community meetings, surveys and women’s focus group discussions.


## Methodology

The gender analysis and action plan was prepared by the feasibility team located in Vanuatu and the GESI adviser based in Australia. The team used a combination of primary and secondary methods:

* Literature review
* Key informant interviews with national government officials including Ministry of Climate Change, Ministry of Agriculture, Livestock, Fisheries, Forests and Biosecurity; Department of Women’s Affairs and Department of Local Authorities.
* Women’s focus group discussions and community surveys in 8 communities across three provinces, using standard question guides and in local languages.

Key informant interviews were conducted with:

* National government officials including Ministry of Climate Change, Ministry of Agriculture, Livestock, Fisheries, Forests and Biosecurity; Department of Women’s Affairs and Department of Local Authorities.
* Key informants identified by involving NGOs.


## 4.0 Findings

National policies on climate change, agriculture, fisheries and gender equality recognize the importance of gender equality and social inclusion in building community climate and disaster resilience.

Translating these policies into service delivery is hampered by limited expertise in gender mainstreaming at national, provincial and local levels of government. Support is needed for area and province level staff to undertake gender and social inclusion analysis with communities and integrating findings into their work plans.

At family level women and men report a high level of shared decision making. Men are seen as heads of households and are responsible for land use decisions in the context of customary land tenure settings. At community level NGOs have supported inclusive decision-making in Community Disaster and Climate Change Committees (CDCCCs) which has created space and shifted norms around participation, leadership roles and gender equality.

Under customary law women’s entitlement to land and natural resources are mediated through husband-wife relationships. This has significant implications for economic development, food security and gender-based violence.

In rural communities while women and men are equally active producers in agriculture and fisheries, the ways of extension and training programs are tailored to the needs and interests of male producers.


---
**Page 132**
---

### Vanuatu Community-based Climate Resilience Project Gender Equality and Women’s Empowerment

- **Women and girls work significantly more hours than men**, with less time for leisure or rest. In rural Tanna women worked twice as many hours as men. Impacts of climate change such as drought, heavy rains, sea level rise and rough seas all increased women's workload.
- **Several women’s groups are active in Vanuatu**. These groups are often family enterprises, with women making up half of rural producers. Engaging families rather than just male heads of household is considered a more effective way of reaching women and men, recognizing and building on the work of all family members.
- **Already high levels of gender-based violence are amplified in the aftermath of disasters**, due to displacement, loss of livelihoods, lack of access to services and resources, and limited access to water.

#### Key Data

| Key Indicator | Value |
| --- | --- |
| Population of Vanuatu is 272,459 (Male 138, 265/Female 134,194) | - |
| Annual population growth rate is 2.3%, one of the highest rates in the region | - |
| Vanuatu has a low representation of women in Parliament | - |
| Vanuatu’s HDI score is 0.659 (2019) which put the country in the medium human development category | - |
| The Gender Development Index (GDI) for Vanuatu is 0.483, which means that women are 52% behind men in terms of GDI | - |
| Vanuatu’s HDI increased by 6.1% (Vanuatu Human Development Report). The Gender Development Index which provides the sex-disaggregated data of the three dimensions measured in the HDI was not able to be calculated due to lack of data on women’s education and employment, as well as men’s education that has not been measured | - |

#### Demographics

- **The population is young**, with 39% under 15 years of age, and 26% aged 15–29. Life expectancy for women is 72.2 years and 69 years for men.
- Three-quarters of the population live in rural areas and depend on agriculture, marine and forest resources.
- **12.7% of the population live below the National Poverty Line**, 57% of people below the poverty line live in rural areas compared to only 30% living in urban areas.

#### Disability Prevalence

- The disability prevalence rate is estimated at 2.6%, with 22% of the population reporting some level of difficulty in functioning.
- Rates of disability are higher in rural areas and increase with age. There is no data on the number of people who have a disability but cannot work.

#### Infant Mortality

- The infant mortality (death/1,000 live births) was 287.6 in children under five years old.
- The child mortality rate (deaths/1,000 live births) for children aged 5–14 years is 39.

#### Child Labour

- The child labour rate is 72,100,000 hours per year, of which 86% are female.
- 65% of those in child labour are engaged in physical or violent work.

#### Literacy Rates

- The literacy rate is 88.3% for men, 86.3% for women. 80% of women in rural areas reported being literate compared with 96% of urban women.
- People with disability are less than half as likely to have completed primary education and only one-third as likely to be literate when compared with people without disability.

#### Education

- The net enrolment rate for children aged six–14 years old is 85.3%, with 90% completing the primary level and 10.1% at the secondary level (UNICEF 2016). The 2016 Mini Census reported 11% of children aged 6–13 years were not attending school and most of these children were in rural areas.

### Laws, Policy and Institutional Arrangements

#### The Constitution of Vanuatu (1980)
States that people have the right to live freely and without discrimination on the grounds of sex, race, place of origin, religion or traditional beliefs, political opinions, language, property ownership, social status, marital status, age, disability, sexual orientation, gender identity, or association with persons and members of underprivileged groups and inhabitants of less developed areas. Vanuatu is signatory to the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (ratified 1995); The Convention on the Rights of the Child (ratified 1993) and Convention on the Rights of People with Disability (ratified 2008). In 2011 Vanuatu signed the United Nations joint statement on gender equality and women's empowerment. Vanuatu is also signatory to the Pacific Leaders Gender Equality Declaration (2015) and the Pacific Leaders' Statement on Women's Empowerment (2016).

#### National Sustainable Development Plan (2018 – 2030)
Sets out specific policy objectives for gender equality and social inclusion in education (2.1), health (3.1), governance planning and budgeting (4.1), protection from violence and discrimination (4.2), and empowering women with disabilities and improving access to services.

#### The country is preparing to launch the second National Gender Equality Policy (NGEP 2020 – 2030)
Aligns gender equality objectives with the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) and national development priorities, including the objective of enhancing women's economic status; mainstreaming gender equality into national sectoral policies (and planning to some extent); and advancing efforts to reduce gender-based violence and increasing women’s participation in decision-making processes. The NGEP 2020-30 will be implemented as a strategic area: Fostering gender-responsive and community-driven solutions to climate and disaster resilience, with a focus on enhancing the capacity of women and girls for climate change adaptation (3.1.4); increasing capacity of climate change and disaster governance bodies (CCDCCs), area and local government.

#### Priority actions in the NGEP relevant to building climate and disaster resilience cover job creation and skills development for women in agriculture, fisheries, handicrafts and tourism, including women with disabilities; promoting gender equality in emergency response planning and implementation (3.2.1); increasing women’s participation in decision making processes and leadership in emergency preparedness and response at community and household level is an effective entry point for shifting social norms and practices.
