# Vamulu Community-based Climate Resilience Project Gender Equality and Social Norms Analysis

## Provincial councils to plan for and respond to needs of vulnerable groups (5.1.3); allocation of budgets for gender analysis and specific gender activities (5.1.3); Funding and supporting women led initiatives that contribute to climate change adaptation, disaster risk reduction and social inclusion outcomes in burles and productive sector recovery (5.3.3). Climate gender action plans should be developed with specific priorities drawn from the NEGP.

### 9. Implementation of the National Inclusive Development Policy (2018 – 2025) commits to mainstreaming the Rights of Persons with Disabilities to ensure that development efforts meet the particular needs and interests of people with disabilities, including women and girls with disabilities. The policy also commits to improving data collection, coordination between sectors, enhancing access, raising awareness of the rights of people with disabilities and providing support specifically to women and girls with disabilities.

### 10. To date the Ministry of Justice and Community Services has lead responsibility for women children and vulnerable groups. The Ministry is responsible for gender equality and inclusion in climate change and disaster risk reduction. However, there are limited resources. Annual budgets only cover office running costs and technical staff salaries. The number of staff working on gender equality and inclusion is limited. DIVA has 14 staff including one in each province. DIVA plays a convening role across the desks including leading the Gender Equality and Inclusion Desk. The Cluster is active at both peaptime and response and with the establishment of DIVA in provinces efforts to hold regular meetings are being strengthened.

### 11. Sectoral policies for climate change and disaster risk reduction (CCDRR Policy) agriculture, fisheries, livestock and forestry all include language on gender equality and inclusion. The CCDRR policy specifically includes a reference to the role of women in adaptation and resilience building. The Climate Change Committees (CCCDCs). Other policies address extension and training services, representation of women in disaster risk reduction and climate change activities. The Ministry of Agriculture and Livestock identified five priority ministries for gender-based budgeting: agriculture fisheries, climate change and disaster risk reduction.

### 12. The legal and policy framework provides a solid basis and direction for mainstreaming gender equality and social inclusion in climate and disaster resilience action. The CCDRR, agriculture, fisheries, forestry and livestock policies all include references to the role of women in adaptation and resilience building or set indicators or targets to generate action and accountability. A recent FAO assessment of the enabling environment for gender equality and women’s empowerment in Agriculture, Forestry and Biosecurity (MALFFB) found that there is a substantial staff interest in understanding more about gender equality and inclusion. The Ministry has established a Gender Equality and Inclusion Desk at the College, which neither of which have specific programmes targeting women/men or people with disabilities.

### 13. Support is required for departments to translate policy into practice and accelerate gender-based planning and action. This includes support in terms of capacity building, resources and funding to support departments to undertake gender and social inclusion analysis and integrate findings into annual planning processes, reporting at national and subnational levels.

## Social Norms and Beliefs

### 14. Social norms and expectations shape the ways in which individuals can access information about climate change adaptation and disaster risk reduction. In Vamulu social norms give men control over women’s behaviour and privilege male decision making in traditional formal, community settings. Gender equality is often perceived and continues to be accepted as a way of enforcing existing power and exerting power. 29% of women with disabilities face stigma and isolation underpinned by negative beliefs and shame about disability.


---
**Page 135**
---

15. In rural areas, kastom governance is the main form of community organisation and social protection (Vanuatu has no non-contributory formal social welfare programs).^1^ Kastom is usually patrilineal with men holding most power in decision making. Women are often excluded from this process as they live on islands and communities are primarily involved above individuals in decision making. Beliefs and practices vary widely between islands but are widely observed and co-exist with people's strong faith in Christianity. Kastom has changed over time, particularly since the introduction of formal education and the implementation of the NGEP 2020. 'Whilst kastom bestowed authority to men with chiefly titles, decisions were shared and agreed upon by all members of a community.'^2^ The NGEP 2020 recognises this and has introduced decision-making no longer exist or constitute modern notions of kastom.^3^ Community profiles undertake the role of monitoring and reporting on the progress of the NGEP 2020. They are also responsible for reporting more shared decision making.

16. Social norms towards early marriage, childbearing and childlessness are persistent especially in rural areas: 22% of women aged between 15-49 years were first married or on union before age 18;^4^ 17% of girls in rural areas (15+ years) have started childbearing compared with 12% in urban areas.^5^ Opportunities for education, employment and social inclusion are limited. Women who marry young are more likely to live away from their families and move to a different community or island, away from their husband's family. This is particularly the case when women marry men older than themselves and have sons; leaving women vulnerable if she is unmarried, the relationship breaks down or her husband dies. Women with disabilities are three times more likely to be widowed, divorced or separated as compared with women with disability and nine times more likely to have never married as compared with women without disability.^6^ This has direct impact on access to land and resources for women.

17. There are significant taboos associated with menstruation; internalised by women and girls including the belief that they should not go near water, eat certain foods or touch other people. Women must avoid cooking food or lift heavy objects. They must also collect their own water for bathing and washing their reusable menstrual products, wash their own menstrual product and use separate toilets and bathing shelters.

18. People with disability in Vanuatu face significant stigma and isolation due to negative social norms and beliefs. Many people with disabilities are unable to attend school or go to work because they cannot access schools or workplaces. Children experience intellectual disability.^7^ Women with disability are often excluded from education, particularly when they have a child. They may be unable to attend school or go to work as they need to care for their children and household duties. People with disabilities are not targeted by food security, water security and livelihoods and are unable to access mainstream opportunities. Vanuatu has no non-contributory social welfare programs.

Roles, Responsibilities and Time Use

19. Significant differences exist in the roles and responsibilities for women and men and girls boys. Rural families operate as a family unit to undertake productive work. Women are expected to care for all children within their household and also take on domestic duties such as cooking, cleaning, washing clothes, weaving mats and handcrafts, sell produce at markets and also provide support to local community and church. Men are expected to be the main breadwinners of the family and undertake productive work in agriculture, fishing and other income generating activities. Women participate in community and church activity. Both women and men are engaged in agriculture in almost equal numbers though they may grow different crops and use different levels of technology. Women generally take on 'heavier' roles of clearing and ploughing land including use of machinery. Fishing in deeper water with larger nets is usually the responsibility of men, whereas women fish for smaller fish using hand lines and fishing from canoes for food security. Women also participate in aquaculture and fish processing to generate income. Men are responsible for collecting firewood and fetching water, while women collect water, neglecting the role of women who harvest close to shore. This both undermines the impact of

### Women’s Community-Based Climate Resilience Project Gender Analysis and Action Plan

#### Fishing pressure along the coast and underestimates the contributions of women.

* Few fisheries programs benefit women or transformed gender relations, and inclusive approaches are hampered by limited knowledge and capacity of fisheries managers and institutional cultures.

#### 20. The project will focus on women’s roles in fishing communities as people without disability but are less likely to be employed outside the home due to barriers to education, training, and employment.
* Information on specific roles of people with disability in agriculture or fisheries in Vanuatu is not available.

#### 21. Overall women and girls work significantly more hours than men: they are twice as likely to leave their role at VPA [Vanuatu Public Authority] jobs early due to pregnancy, and show greater resilience when faced with a major crisis.
* Women faced with this data in the community were surprised and many indicated a willingness to take on more responsibility. This is particularly true for women who have lost family members or are widowed.

#### 22. Labour migration is an increasingly important feature of rural livelihoods as the need for cash grows. Most labour migrants are young men, but women also migrate in search of better opportunities.
* Women and girls’ roles and workloads restrict access to training and extension initiatives that could assist with adaptation strategies such as livestock diversification.

#### 23. Women’s and girls’ roles and workloads restrict access to training and extension initiatives that could assist with adaptation strategies such as livestock diversification.
* When family incomes are under pressure the needs of women and girls increase, particularly in relation to food security, education, health, and safety.
* Women and girls face barriers to accessing information on rehabilitation and healthcare, withdrawal of girls from school and early marriage. While primary and secondary schools have improved access for girls, on average girls still have less ability to participate further in education and training is restricted.

#### 24. Agricultural extension services and enterprise development can reach a local level but need to be adapted to recognize that rural livelihoods are family enterprises, with women taking up half of rural production time.
* Women’s workloads are often increased by their role over men’s work. Service providers need to shift approach to recognize that rural livelihoods are family enterprises, with women taking up half of rural production time and the burden of care for children and elderly people.
* The project will focus on women’s roles in fishing communities as people without disability but are less likely to be employed outside the home due to barriers to education, training, and employment.

#### 25. Family farm teams approach can address the increased risks of gender-based violence that women can face when they increase their incomes.
* Building on women’s existing roles including targeting capacity support to women in markets and in women’s church groups are also entry points.

Decision Making, leadership and participation

25 Decision making is male dominated in Vanuatu. Since independence in 1980 only five women have been elected to Vanuatu's national parliament, and there are no women in Parliament in 2021. While women have consistently been underrepresented at the local level, the introduction of a quota system introduced in 2013 has led to increases in the number of women elected or appointed to Municipal Councils (MC) and Village Councils (VC). In 2021 there were no women MC members but some are women mayors. There are some women deputy mayor roles. In the public sector women are better represented at the lower levels, with 45% of staff in the Department of Health (DoH), Ministry of Education and Training (MoET) and Ministry of Community Services, Youth and Sports (MCSYS). At the international level women have led Vanuatu delegations to COP21 (2015), COP23 (2017) and 2019 and women's representation in delegations has been 30-50% since 2017.

26 Social norms about masculinity and leadership are slowly changing. At community level male chiefs have historically dominated decision making, but the Cluster on Disaster Risk Management (DRM), gender and village development provide a framework for women, people with disability and youth to participate and take an active role in disaster risk reduction and climate change adaptation activities. The increasing number of women, people with disability and youth have increased community acceptance of their leadership roles.

27 The Vanuatu Gender and protection cluster was established in 2014 and has built a strong network of government, international and national NGO partners focused on disaster management, as well as issues related to gender equality, human rights and social inclusion. The Cluster is led by the DoH and includes subcommittees on children, leadership and disability. The cluster has been an important venue for building capacity in gender equality and women's empowerment through training and response. Challenges faced by the Cluster include securing financing from government for program implementation, ensuring that mainstreaming of other clusters and building provincial cluster mechanisms. There is a risk that action on gender equality will be subsumed into the operations of other clusters or DWA, rather than embedded into the operations of all clusters, provincial and municipal emergency operations.

28 Women have low levels of representation in the formal private sector but are active in small and medium enterprises (SMEs). In some areas women's groups, cooperatives and savings and credit unions and savings cooperatives have large numbers of women members including in leadership roles. These provide an important entry point for business skills development (eg UN Women Markets for Change, VAWA) and can be a vehicle to support women's access to finance. They are also important for climate adaptation and resilience building. While women in most communities are excluded from kastom leadership roles they may play an active part in other areas such as the leadership of women's groups and youth groups.

29 At the household level men are dominant at the head of households. In the FAD survey, 80% of women respondents were selected by men and together with men but that was not the final decision makers. Women in Focus Groups in North Amphi and Epi, North West Santo women reported that most decisions about land use and resource management were made by men. A participatory approach is important to reinforce existing shared decision making, instead of privileging male decision making.

30 People with disability report isolation and stigma, as well lack of accessibility as barriers that prevent participation in community events and committees. Examples include COCOCC meetings being held in "open" areas where people with disability cannot access the venue. Women with disabilities are often teachers take on the role of representing women with disability instead of communities supporting people with disability to participate. The Vanuatu National Council for People with Disability, Advocacy and Advisory Association, Vanuatu Society for People with Disability and Rainbow Theatre (Wow Small Bag) are the main organizations working in this area. There is a need to increase awareness about disability recognition by government and donors that data systems needed to improve to better support people with disabilities. The Cluster has been working on this issue since 2014. In 2019, 35% of disaster training for people with disability as trainers, and 42% of industry trainers in the productive sectors are women.


---
**Page 138**
---

Access to and Control of Resources

31. **97%** of land in Vanuatu is under customary ownership which is largely patrilineal. Men have control over land, marine and forest resources, while women have access or use rights to natural resources in line with established cultural norms, such as fishing, hunting, gathering, and the right to collect firewood for domestic purposes or during pregnancy [4]. On marriage women move to their husband’s community or island and access to resources is determined by the husband. Women are also more likely than men to be excluded from land titles and resources. Widows and female headed households also face exclusion or marginal access to natural resources.

32. Women in rural areas earn income through marketing crops and fish, processing copra, cooking food and handicrafts. Fewer than one in five women have a bank account, though village loans and savings schemes are available for women. Women’s groups are often the only source of credit for women to start businesses and as collateral women struggle to access larger loans [4]. Women’s focus group discussions identified a priority need for financial services.

33. Small and medium enterprise development provides an opportunity to diversify livelihoods and also integrate climate and disaster resilience into existing enterprises such as handicrafts. It is important that enterprises are linked with markets, particularly in the context of climate change. This could include linking RDI delivery partners to work with market development programs [4].

34. People with disability have low access to assistive devices and rehabilitation services; less than 20% have received a wheelchair or other mobility aid while only about one out of half of the people who needed services had received them.

35. Access to agricultural inputs such as seeds and marine machinery is gendered in many countries, including Vanuatu, with women having less access than men to tools, machinery such as grass cutters, food processing equipment like grinders, tractors, stoves or generators [4]. Improved irrigation systems, fertilizer, drying and processing of crops, machines, improved livestock contribute to adaptability capacity. It will be important to ensure these technologies are available to women as well as men, and that women have the skills necessary for their use in order to maximize benefits from climate change adaptation technologies.

36. Improvements in household food production is an area to target. Childhood nutrition data in Vanuatu shows a quarter of children are stunted and Women’s Focus Groups identified changing rainfall as impacting on the variety of foods they can grow and families can eat, with a drop in leafy greens and vegetables [4]. Traditional food preparation methods such as smoking, drying and preserving have long term benefits however this food is also attractive as it reduces women's time burden. Similarly traditional food preservation methods such as fermentation are also important for the health of children but also have implications on women’s work burden. Adaptive technology that also reduces women’s unpaid care worksloads is more likely to be adopted.

37. Access to mobile phones is a significant asset in Vanuatu with mobile networks improving communications and putting communication into the hands of individuals. Most households have a mobile phone, however only 10% of rural women own one [4]. The Women’s Empowerment Project (WEP) initiative – Women Wema Wesa – has established a mobile SMS network translating weather and health warnings to women in their local language. This is an innovative approach that uses existing infrastructure low bandwidth SMS and social media to maximise reach. These women are then responsible for sharing information with other women within the community, particularly those who may not have access or those with disabilities in their community. To start the initiative rural women leaders were given phones and data credit as well as training on how to use these resources themselves. The effective use of technology and data builds on women’s social roles, while empowering them with critical information. This initiative is not operational in all project areas.

Gender-Based Violence

38. Vanuatu has a high prevalence of gender-based violence (GBV), in particular violence against women and girls. In 2011, research showed that **60%** of women have experienced some form of physical and/or


---
**Page 139**
---

# Violence

Violence is seen as an acceptable way of controlling behavior and enforcing expectations on women and children. In rural settings, social norms and kastom decision making by village elders often override laws and policies about violence against women and children.

While the Family Protection Act was passed in 2016 for services to victims of gender-based violence are limited with little available in rural areas and low usage of protection orders. In a 2015 study, 74% of women reported having experienced physical or sexual violence by an intimate partner, husband, or chief, and only 6% of had used one. In 2020, a free telephone hotline service was established to provide counseling services for survivors of gender-based violence.

## Violence against Women

39. Sexual and physical violence is also high among women and girls with disabilities, both physical and mental. Family members are often attainted to leave women and girls with disabilities alone in the home for fear of sexual abuse or other forms of violence.

40. In the aftermath of disasters GBV is amplified. Where families lose homes and are displaced, lack of safe housing increases vulnerability to violence. Women and girls are more likely than men to be displaced from their homes and to seek shelter with family members in unsafe conditions. Displacement also limits access in health and GBV support services, transport routes and policing prevent women and girls from seeking assistance. Loss of food crops and stores have been demonstrated as contributing to increased gender-based violence against women and girls. In addition, climate change impacts on water availability and quality impacts on water sources and agriculture mean women and girls need to travel further to farm and collect water, increasing their vulnerability to sexual harassment and assault.

41. The COVID-19 pandemic has also amplified GBV in the aftermath of disasters. Lockdowns and curfews surge block transport routes preventing pregnant and lactating women from accessing essential health services. Women are more likely than men to be displaced by climate change, conflict or natural disasters facilities. There is ever-growing evidence that climate-induced disasters exacerbate early child marriage, domestic violence, sexual exploitation and abuse of children, and gender-based violence as poverty and social norms that value community cohesion over the best interests of girls, there is growing evidence that climate change impacts on water availability and quality impacts on water sources and agriculture mean women and girls need to travel further to farm and collect water, increasing their vulnerability to sexual harassment and assault.

42. Gender and protection assessments following TC Hanold (and with COVID19) identified common priorities:

- Increasing GBV support services and policing.
- Ensuring chiefs and service providers have an understanding of GBV risks after disasters
- Improving access to health, material and sexual and reproductive health services
- Putting child protection measures in place
- Psychological first aid
- Improving women's leadership role in CDCDC, provincial emergency operations, women, church and community groups.

43. Research following TC Pam showed the effects of the disaster are amplified for persons with disability – they were more likely to be displaced from their homes and to seek shelter with family members in unsafe conditions. Some had no access to mobile phones or other communication devices and some were lost in TC Pam. Adults with disability also had less access to DRR efforts, with women more excluded than men. Women with disability also had less access to response and recovery services.

### North Ambrity Area Council: Malampa

There are slightly more women (55%) than men in each community of 250 – 350 people and all communities have a population density of less than 100 people per km². All communities have active chiefs who organise community work. All have village development councils and plan for the future. Most communities have a women’s group, a youth group, a savings and credit committee, some have a health committee, market committees and savings groups. All have CDCCCs, with women as chairpersons or members of the executive committee. Women are also involved in the secretaries of some committees but do not chair committees. The main income sources are kava, livestock, fish and local produce sales. Women also earn money through banking. For all communities the Area council and administration was seen as working well with them, but they had little contact with provincial or national departments.

### Ramu community

The village has a market house but limited access to banking. Women do not have access to microloans, but have, with men attended financial literacy training. The community has good access to health services and women are involved in the secretaries of some committees but do not chair them. Cyclones and volcanic ashfalls. Women identified contamination of water, water borne illness, food crop production, lack of clean water, lack of sanitation, lack of shelter, lack of transport and disasters. The community faces food security challenges due to cyclones, overuse and degradation of land. There is good connection to the Digital network and hand-held radios, but most not all households have access to electricity. Women are involved in the secretaries of some committees but do not chair them. Access to DRR and climate change where women and youth participated however awareness of risks and adaptation measures was low. The community has a water committee that is active and women have access to safe shelter during cyclones, though these do not have private washroom facilities or emergency shelters. Women are involved in the secretaries of some committees but do not chair them. There is no funding for CDCCC work. Women in the community are active in the VDCs in roles of secretary and treasurer. They generally feel respected by men. Additional to family agriculture/fishing they earn money.


---
**Page 141**

### Magam Community

45. Cyclones, droughts, and volcanic ash are key concerns identified in this community. Cyclones have damaged buildings and water systems, creating food and water shortages for 3-4 months a year and water has to be collected from 1km away; there is no water committee in the community. Heavy rain erodes soils and volcanic ash affects gardens and water sources. Damage to roads and buildings from cyclones can take up to two years to repair, leading to food and water shortages for longer periods after disasters, and women go to the Chief to seek support for abuse. Most women in the community are able to speak English but some have difficulty communicating with others due to language barriers. There is no one with disabilities on the CDCCD. The community does not have a disaster/vaccination and CCDDR plan with support from Vodafone microfinance. Women can access mobile phones, but only 30% of women in the community have access to the Vodafone network. Decisions about visiting the health centre, small and large household purchases, and other decisions are made by men together. Less than half of the women have access to microloans.

51. There are concrete buildings used as cyclone shelters, but do not have private toilet facilities for women and men. After disaster events women noted the level of conflict and family violence is about the same, and the same reasons for conflict exist (e.g., lack of food, water). Women in the community have access to microloans and have attended financial literacy training. There are no community funds for cyclone shelters or other disasters. The community has a Vodafone network and they get warnings and weather information from VMGD through social media. Women can access mobile phones but only 30% of women in the community have access to the Vodafone network (and with support from Vodafone microfinance). Decisions about visiting the health centre, small and large household purchases, while large purchases and visits to family outside the community are decided by women and men together. Women earn money through baking bread and donuts.

53. Priorities identified in Women's Focus Group: assistance with food security and financial literacy.

There is no water committee in the community. Heavy rain erodes soils and volcanic ash affects gardens and water sources. ### Vanuatu Area Council, Epi Island, Shefa Province

47. In this area there is a population of 10-20 households and range from 280 – 333 people, with 55% women, and aged 5-55 years old. 3-5% have a lot of difficulties in functioning across one or more of six areas. All communities are vulnerable to cyclones, droughts, volcanic ash, and floods. Most communities have between 2 and 6 development plans. All communities have women's groups, church groups, youth groups and water, health and market committees. Two communities have micro-finance or savings groups, and women can access mobile phones but only 30% of women in the community have access to the Vodafone network and copra sales. There are no functional CDCCCDs, evacuation or climate adaptation plans or access to clean water.

## Nyalapalepi (community)

This community is affected by cyclones, drought, high seas and volcanic ash fall. Roads and schools have been damaged during flooding roads and paths are impossible. Women identify population growth straining resources, inadequate access to health facilities and water as major problems. Water problems are exacerbated by lack of storage capacity. Lack of water storage is leading to low vegetable production due to poor nutrition and food security. Women are also concerned about loss of housing during disaster events.

### 62. Some women are able to speak and share ideas at community meetings, and there is a woman and girls church group and handcrafts group. The village council has 3 men and 5 women and a women chair the school committee. Women earn money through weaving and handicrafts, sale of cooked food.

## Mani Wan Community

This area is affected by cyclones, flooding, drought and volcanic activity. There are extremely rough seas and storm surges make roads impassable. Heavy rains damage food gardens and make it difficult to collect water for cooking and drinking. Women identify lack of access to health facilities as a major problem and women also identified illness, income security, food security, water security as risks associated with cyclones. Lack of storage capacity is leading to low vegetable production due to poor nutrition and food security. Women are able to speak and share ideas at community meetings and there is a women's church group. There are no women in the village council or other community groups. Women earn money through weaving and handicrafts, sale of cooked food. Women go to the chief, church and family for support regarding abuse or domestic conflict and they also seek help from the police. The village has a mobile phone but only 10% of households have mobile phones none receive climate, weather or disaster information. Women are able to decide themselves on spending money outside the community and men and women decide together about household purchases.

## Priorities identified in Women's Focus Group: Training in DRR, personal finance, Climate Change Adaptation, Disaster Risk Reduction

### 50. Women prioritize in Women’s Focus Group: Training in DRR, personal finance, Climate Change Adaptation, Disaster Risk Reduction

# North West Area (Santa Elena, Santa Cruz Province)

## Ocopa Community

### Priority Identified in Women’s Focus Group: Weaving, savings & loan programs, first aid training, CRR training, income generating activities, training women selling produce at markets.

### Penamasu Community

The Penamasu faces drought, fire and storms causing river flooding which has destroyed houses, fruit and cash crops. They experience water shortages throughout the year and have few improved toilets. Thirteen women are active in health (health/postnatal care), while 10 women are active in income generating activities such as sale of copra, cocoa and agricultural products in Luganville. They have received small business management training from women’s groups. The community has a good relationship with the area administrator and radio Vanuatu is available at night only. Women are concerned that they cannot receive information about climate and weather forecasts or disaster warning. Women are active themselves about small and large household purchases and visiting outside the community, while men are worried together about visiting health care. The community raises the relationship with area administration as poor.

### Priority Identified in Women’s Focus Group: Weaving, savings & loan programs, first aid training, CRR training, income generating activities, training women selling produce at markets.

# Vanuatu Community-based Climate Resilience Project

## Project specific conclusions and recommendations

72. It is important that the project benefits women, people with disability and youth, as well as men, and that poverty reduction strategies and community resilience are transformed. Project interventions should support women, people with disability and youth to access climate information, and resources and skills to build their capacity in disaster risk management. This will help them challenge discriminatory norms and behaviours associated with work roles and workload, leadership and violence, including child marriage and early pregnancy. Women, people with disability and youth need to be supported for improved activities for women, people with disability and youth to also lift women's voice and agency and transformation power dynamics at household, community, area and province levels that serve to reinforce inequality.

73. A 'twin track' approach to disability inclusion is needed – working directly with people with disability and their families, as well as through the wider community. The Vanuatu Association of People with Disability (VAPD), Vanuatu Society of Disabled People and Vanuatu Skills Partnership are key partners for building skills and capacity in CDDCCs. The National Council of Disabled People's Association (Saniti) and Rainbow Theatre are important advocacy organisations.

### Household and Community Level

74. At the household and community level the following approaches should be prioritised:

- Families with women as household head and engage male and female heads of household in planning and implementation of adaptation actions to ensure that women’s and men's roles in agriculture and fisheries are balanced, and that women have access to land and resources for their livelihoods. This will reduce unpaid work and sharing workload equally. Modules address family goal setting and finances; planning and implementing adaptation actions; gender equality and decision making; and conflict and decision making in the family. Working at this level will also enable identification of family members with disability, to link with disability inclusive skills/training, as well as participation in CDDCCs.
- Ensure that women and youth are included in disaster risk reduction activities and emergency response, including shelters and evacuation centres, to reduce violence.
- Emergency shelters should be prioritised for accessibility and wash facilities. Infrastructure projects should include ramps, toilets, water points and lighting. This will ensure that women with disability have training of women, youth and people with disability and opportunities for work at equal pay with men. Codes of conduct for disaster response teams and survivor-centred response should be available on any GBV issues raised through building processes.

### CDDCC

- Train all CDDCCs on disability inclusive DRR
  - Identify and support women with disability in the community to be represented on the CDDCC public committee.
  - Ensure women and youth are represented on the CDDCC, including 50% target for women's representation.
  - Provide targeted training and coaching support to women and people with disability to build confidence and skills.
  - Provide training to CDDCC members on women’s empowerment and GBV (e.g. roles, norms and values that determine what men and women do, how men and women provide food, read to and educate children, manage household finances, make decisions about family planning and child marriage and forced unions) and disability.
  - Emergency response plans should clearly identify people at higher risk and target support to ensuring their safety. CDDCCs should be trained on rapid needs assessments clearly identifying needs of women, children, elderly and people with disabilities.

- Work with the media to raise awareness about gender equality and increase awareness on the impacts of societal inequality on poverty and climate change and change attitudes and beliefs around gender roles and responsibilities and negative attitudes about the capabilities of women, youth and people with disability in the community.


---
**Page 145**
---

### Improving linkages and coordination between the provincial and area levels are important for gender and social inclusion particularly as government staff from the Department for Women’s Affairs (DWA) and NGOs can be deployed to support the Provincial Disaster Management Project.

- **Support implementation of provincial gender action plans for training Provincial Disaster committees in gender and protection issues, supporting women-led initiatives, and supporting representation and leadership opportunities for women; and providing livelihoods training for women.**

- **Building DWA staff capacity at province level to coordinate across government and NGOs, including the general coordination of disaster response, mainstreaming gender and social inclusion into province and area operations for MALFBB and MoCC.**


---
**Page 146**
---

* Allocate funding to enable provincial Department for Women staff to travel to and work with area administration on gender focused adaptation activities including GED training for CCDCCs, women's leadership development and women-led adaptation initiatives.
* Implement a multi-level Early Warning System (EWS) to ensure that Early Warning Systems (EWS) reach area and community level, and information (including rapid needs assessment) from communities is shared with provincial authorities in a timely manner.
* Maintain accurate data about people with disability to enable service delivery and disaster preparedness and response is a priority.

**Major VCCRPs**: The VCCRP, VSDP, VDPPA, VSVPTRA and VANVODOS have provincial operations, with established relationships with provincial, area and community leaders. VCCRP should be the lead organisation as project partners as they have a track record and critical relationships in each province.

**Project Operations**

77. It is important to promote the value that gender equality and social inclusion is core to project effectiveness – if we care about community resilience then everyone’s needs are important, and everyone's contributions need to be recognised. This should be articulated by project leadership and through project communications.

78. Mainstreaming targets should be set across the project operations to drive accountability:
* 50% female staff
* targets for women in leadership positions
* targets for recruitment, training and promotion of people with disability.
* All project staff should be trained in gender equality and social inclusion

Findings from this report should be disseminated to SC staff and partners.

Please note that the following recommendations are based on the findings of this report. They understand and address the needs of diverse groups:

Employ at least one full time GES technical advisor and additional technical support on disability inclusion, with experience in analysis, planning and monitoring. The advisors must be able to mentor and train government counterparts at MoCCD, MAFLLF and provide DIVA staff and have budget to travel between provinces.

Project communications should be designed to challenge social norms. For example, by using appropriate language; and showing women of all ages and people with disability in active leadership roles.

MEAL activities should track changes in knowledge, attitudes, perceptions and behaviors related to gender equality and social inclusion.

Regular review of GES Action Plan, and adaptations made to Action Plan and project implementation as required.

79. Implementation arrangements should engage partners with a track record of working with communities in each particular province. Expertise in gender equality and disability inclusion may need to be centralised through the VCCRP or other organisations such as the Children’s Centre for Gender Equality, the Children's Centre for support on gender-based violence, working with men and boys, relationship building; Save the Children, UNICEF, etc.

80. Referral pathways and relationships should be established with other organisations to deal with specific issues eg VANVODOS access to finance, savings and loans groups and enterprise skills development; VCCRP for child protection services; Area and provincial health services, especially for access to reproductive and sexual health services.

# Vanuatu Community-based Climate Resilience Project Gender Assessment and Action Plan

## Project Communications
- Findings of the GESI assessment will be disseminated to project staff, government and partners in Year 1 of implementation.
- Project communications will include women, youth and people with disability in positive, empowering ways. They will use local language and deliver in accessible formats.

The project team will ensure that the communication mechanism through means that ensure the voices of all affected groups are heard and valued.
- When making decisions about the locations and recipients of the interventions, the project will ensure accessibility to all affected stakeholders, including women and children, and that it responds effectively to SEAM in a survivor-centred and gender-responsive manner.

## Community level considerations
- Women's time and care work will be considered when determining the timing and location for all community-level activities. Childcare facilities will be provided where required during project implementation.
- The results of the GESI assessment will be communicated to participants prior to commencing any activities.
- Shifting gender norms is a key component of SEAM in this project. Behaviour change communications (utilizing resources such as radio, SMS and social media) will be utilized in community engagement and training.
